FOREWORD
In today’s dynamic healthcare landscape, the quest for quality and uniformity of care at the primary health
care level remains paramount. It is with great pleasure that the Community Health Practitioners Registration
Board of Nigeria [CHPRBN] presents the 2024 revised National Standing Orders for Community Health
Officers (CHOs) It is a comprehensive set of standard operational guidelines meticulously crafted through
the collaborative efforts of the reputable regulatory establishment, principally supported by USAID/Nigeria
through its Health Workforce Management Activity, the Federal Ministry of Health, the National Primary
Health Care Development Agency [NPHCDA], and other Pertinent MDAs.
The significance of t he N ational S tanding Orders c annot b e overstated. A s a g uiding framework that
links Communities and the national health system, it serves as a cornerstone in ensuring consistency and
excellence in healthcare practices across the nation. By providing clear protocols and procedural guidelines,
it empowers Community Health Officers to deliver services o f t he h ighest standard, thereby enhancing
positive Community health outcomes.
Accordingly, the National Standing Orders foster a culture of accountability and professionalism within the
Community Health Profession. By adhering to established guidelines, Community Health Officers uphold
ethical standards and demonstrate their commitment to providing safe and effective care to all individuals,
regardless of their socio-economic status or geographic location.
Furthermore, this document has been painstakingly compiled to serve as a tool for capacity building and
continuous improvement. Through regular updates and revisions, it reflects the latest advancements in
healthcare and best practices, equipping healthcare providers with the knowledge and skills necessary to
adapt to evolving healthcare needs and challenges.
In essence, the National Standing Orders epitomize our collective commitment to advancing healthcare
delivery and safeguarding the well-being of our citizens. It is our fervent hope that this document will serve
as a catalyst for positive change and contribute significantly to the achievement of national health objectives.
Therefore, I strongly endorse the 2024 edition of the National Standing Orders to the Community Health
Officers, to provide quality, affordable, and uniformity of care within the national health system. Predominantly,
I extend our deepest appreciation to all those who have contributed to this review, recognizing their
dedication and expertise in shaping the future of healthcare in Nigeria. It is my ardent hope and trust that
Community Health Officers will appreciate the huge efforts put in place for this review by reciprocating with
strict adherence to this national document and demonstrating a high sense of responsibility.
Dr.Tunji Alausa
Hon. Minister of state for Health
August,2024
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS

ACKNOWLEDGEMENT
The review and production of the 2024 edition were made possible by USAID/Nigeria through its Health
Workforce Management Activity. The regulatory establishment [CHPRBN] reviewed this document through
collaborative support from the Federal Ministry of Health, the National Primary Health Care Development
Agency [NPHCDA], and other critical stakeholders. The Board acknowledged all the sources of materials
used in this publication.
The main objective of the National Standing Orders is to ensure quality, affordable, and uniform care, especially
at the Primary Health Care level, provided by Community Health Practitioners. The review committee
deserves a high level of commendation for the effort they put into the review process of this important
document. Their dedication and collaboration have been instrumental in achieving the overarching objective
of ensuring quality, affordable, and uniform care for individuals, families, and communities.
In light of the foregoing and the spirit of collaboration, I am pleased to extend sincere heartfelt appreciation
to all contributors to this revised edition. Indeed, your invaluable contributions will undoubtedly benefit
countless individuals and communities in Nigeria. Your partnership exemplifies the spirit of collective effort
in advancing healthcare delivery and promoting the well-being of our dear citizens.
Dr. Bashir Idris, RCHP, FIPHP [Li idin Jama’a]
Registrar/CEO

NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 15
INTRODUCTION
The National Standing Orders serve as a comprehensive set of specific guidelines, arranged by age group,
conditions, findings (from history, physical examination, laboratory investigations), clinical judgment, and
necessary actions, to standardize care for patients at Primary Health Care (PHC) Facilities. Developed
through extensive collaboration between Community Health Practitioners’ Registration Board of Nigeria
(CHPRBN) and the National Primary Health Care Development Agency (NPHCDA), with support from the
United States Agency for International Development (USAID), these comprehensive guidelines equip health
workers with standardized procedures and protocols for managing a wide range of health conditions. The
objectives of the Standing Orders are to:
•
•
•
•
•
•
Ensure legal protection for health workers in their primary health care duties.
Offer a structured approach for history taking and physical examination.
Aid in the management of less common, easily overlooked, and more serious conditions.
Maintain a consistently high standard of healthcare.
Minimize unnecessary, expensive, and time-consuming laboratory investigations.
Provide a framework for evaluating care and staff performance.
Following the guidelines will better position the Community Health Practitioners at the frontline to provide
quality care that is Safe, Timely, Equitable, Effective, Effective, and Person-centered.
WHAT YOU WILL FIND
•
•
Clear and Practical Guidelines: Organized by age groups and specific conditions, the
Standing Orders provide step-by-step instructions for history taking, physical examination,
treatment plans, and follow-up care. This user-friendly format ensures consistent and highquality care for all patients.
A framework to manage common and less frequent conditions.You’ll find valuable guidance
for even complex or life-threatening situations, along with clear referral pathways which
will enhance clinical decision making.
More than just guidelines, these standing orders represent years of field-tested experience and continuous
refinement, making them practical and relevant to the environments where Community Health Professionals
operate. Each complaint or symptom that a patient may present with is addressed with a concise introduction,
providing cues on important aspects, followed by a suggested history, examination, education, further
investigation, treatment, and follow-up to ensure optimal patient care. Simplicity, clarity, and ease of use have
been prioritized, with more common conditions addressed first and more severe conditions prioritized over
moderate and mild ones.
In cases of complicated or life-threatening conditions, the Standing Orders specify referral to the next
level of care or the nearest hospital as appropriate. The uniformity of care provided by these guidelines
facilitates evaluation, setting a standard for care and allowing for the assessment of overall care adequacy and
identification of areas needing improvement.
PAGE 16
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
IMPORTANT NOTE: The Standing orders do not replace clinical judgement and critical thinking. Health
workers must still gather essential history and physical examination details to make informed clinical
decisions and provide appropriate treatment. These protocols delineate the necessary actions and decisions
required to manage a patient’s symptoms effectively.The Community Health Practitioner must ensure proper
documentation of all inferences made and action taken in the management of the patient. By adhering to
these standardized procedures, you can ensure your actions are well-supported and documented, minimizing
legal risks and maximizing patient safety.
The effectiveness of these Standing Orders, like the broader primary health care concept, depend on welltrained, committed, and dedicated health workers. These Standing Orders are therefore complemented by
the curricula developed for use in Schools of Health Technology and Community Health Officers’ Training
Institutions. The continued and proper utilization of these Orders, for both TRAINING AND PRACTICE,
will ensure the sustenance of a high standard of health care in the community. For the 2024 revision of
the Standing Orders, an illustrated version has been created to support the ease of use and improved
understanding of the guidelines.
HOW TO USE THIS GUIDE
The Standing Orders are organized by complaints or problems derived by the health workers from the
patient’s history, an examination, and/or laboratory tests. Follow the steps below in your interaction with
the patient:
1.
2.
3.
4.
5.
6.
Based on the patient’s age group, identify the problems or complaints in the relevant
section and refer to the appropriate page.
Ask all questions listed in the history box.
Perform all examinations listed in the examination box.
Record all significant information, including negative findings. For example, if examining the
neck for stiffness, note the absence of stiffness as “no stiff neck.”
Select the box containing all significant findings related to the patient’s condition from the
list of findings.
Follow the actions corresponding to the selected findings and manage the patient’s
complaints as instructed.
In some cases, it may be necessary to refer to more than one section of the Standing Orders to completely
manage all of the patient’s issues comprehensively.Take appropriate action based on your findings and record
them in the home-based record (PHC Child Health card or green card for children under 5 years and
Personal Health Record yellow card for those over 5 years old) or in the relevant patient record platform
provided. Review the patient’s condition whenever a course of medicine or injection is due to finish and
based on the medications’ onset of action. This is an excellent way to evaluate your work carefully. Ensure
the home-based record card is returned to the mother/caregiver or patient.
ADDITIONAL INSTRUCTIONS:
•
Every patient should undergo the complete examination listed under the First Visit at
their initial contact with the health facility, except in emergencies, in which case the
treatment should be prioritized and the ‘first visit’ examination should be completed at the
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 17
next visit.
•
•
•
•
Address the health issues of all family members present in the clinic whenever possible.
Follow up on all referred cases when patients return to ensure continuity of care and
facilitate health worker learning.
Advise all patients to return to the health facility immediately if their conditions worsen.
Health education is crucial for patient care. Provide relevant health education to the
appropriate patients on topics such as:
-Breastfeeding (immediate & exclusive) and complementary feeding where
appropriate.
-Nutrition for older children & adults.
-Newborn care at home.
-Child-spacing.
-Personal hygiene.
-Immunization.
-Patient’s presenting condition.
-STD/HIV/AIDS (or other current health concerns).
-Importance of home-based records.
This comprehensive approach ensures that health workers can provide effective and standardized care while
continually improving their practice through ongoing evaluation and education.
Collaborative work: When you are in doubt about the care plan for a patient, consult with other healthcare
workers at the facility or stabilize the patient and refer appropriately. When you do not have the required
resources to manage a patient, promptly refer to the appropriate facility or level of care to avoid delay in the
commencement of care for the patient.
FEEDBACK AND SUPPORT CONTACT
We encourage all Community Health Practitioners to actively participate in the continuous improvement of
this guide. If you encounter any issues, have suggestions for improvements, or notice any outdated information,
please do not hesitate to reach out to us. Your feedback is crucial in maintaining the accuracy and relevance
of this essential resource and provide valuable insights for future revisions.
How To Provide Feedback
Email: Send detailed feedback, suggestions, or corrections to info@chprbn.gov.ng
Please include specific references to sections or illustrations where applicable.
Phone: Contact our support team at 08034462362 (Ibrahim Umar) for immediate assistance or to discuss
your feedback in detail.
Online Form: Please fill out your structured feedback using the google form via this link

SECTION ONE
THE NEWBORN

NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS

SECTION ONE
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
THE NEWBORN
A newborn is defined as a baby aged 0 to 28 days. Properly cared for, they will cry and breathe without
assistance. When a newborn cries, it indicates they are breathing, which is generally a positive sign. As the
baby’s breathing improves, their skin will become pink, a reassuring sign to observe. However, newborns
may also exhibit signs of fatigue or weakness due to the stress of birth or underlying illness. Therefore, it is
essential to carefully monitor newborns and handle them gently with clean hands. Avoiding slapping the baby
on any part of their body is advisable.
Newborns are susceptible to becoming wet and cold easily, so it’s crucial to keep them dry and well wrapped.
Mothers should be encouraged to bring their children for regular visits: monthly during the first two years of
life, every three months until age 5, and as needed thereafter. Additionally, mothers should be encouraged to
immunize their babies as soon as possible and continue until they are fully immunized.
1.1.1 IMMEDIATE CARE OF THE NEWBORN
FINDING ON HISTORY
1. First One Hour
FINDINGS ON
EXAMINATION
1. Normal Newborn

1.1.2 FIRST POSTPARTUM HOME VISIT (2—3 DAYS AFTER
THE BIRTH OF A NEWBORN)
The postnatal period begins immediately after the birth of a newborn, extending to six weeks after delivery.
This period is crucial for the survival of the newborn. Proper care must be taken to avoid complications
including death.The post-natal care visits days are 3, 5, 7, 9, 14, 21, 28 - If the birth weight of the baby is normal
(>2.5kg), at least three home visits are required on days 3, 7 and 14. - If the birth weight of the baby is low.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
Ask for the frequency of newborn breastfeeding (infants eat about
every 1–3 hours)
1.Ask for the number of times the Newborn wets the diaper in a
day (should have about 6 wet diapers a day)
2.Ask for the number of times in a day that the newborn stools
(most newborns have 3 or 4 soft bowel movements a day)
3.Ask for number of times the newborn sleeps in a day (A newborn may sleep 14 to 17 hours or more in 24 hours)
4.Ask for the number of hours that the newborn sleeps between
feedings (Newborns should not sleep more than 4 hours between
feedings until they have good weight gain, usually within the first
few weeks).
Perform comprehensive physical examination (PE) within 24
hours of birth, and within 72 hours of life
1.Provide essential newborn care
2.Assess for Danger signs and refer newborn to the health
facility if any danger sign is seen/observed
3.Check the baby’s weight, length, and head circumference and
plot the measurements on a growth chart.
4.Check if Newborns seem hungry
5.Watch as the mother breastfeeds the newborn and offer

the newborn because it protects the newborn from infection.
10.Promote Exclusive Breast Feeding - Exclusive breastfeeding
means that a baby takes only breast milk with no additional
foods or liquids, not even water (as breast milk is predominantly water), except medicine if necessary, for the first 6
months when complementary feeding begins. Baby should be
breastfed 8 – 12 times per day and on demand.
11.Tell the mother the advantages of breast milk which
include:
-It is the best food for the baby as it digests easily
-It protects newborn from infection
-It promotes bonding between the mother and the newborn
12.Demonstrate good positioning and good attachment and
discuss the importance of breastfeeding
13.Promote Immunization: Newborns are particularly vulnerable to infections. Immunization is an important way to protect
an infant’s health. They prevent illnesses and diseases and
failure to immunize may put the infant at risk of serious illness
or even death
14.Check immunization status and educate on the importance
of completing all the child’s immunizations.

4.Use fragrance-free soaps and lotions.
5.Hold the baby and be attentive to their needs.
6.Sing, talk, and read to your baby and look directly to your
baby’s eyes and smile when breastfeeding. Provide ways for
your baby to see, hear, move arms and legs freely, and to
7.Call your Health care worker if your infant has a fever of
100.4ºF (38ºC),is acting sick, isn’t eating, isn’t peeing or pooping, looks yellow, or has increasing redness or pus around the
umbilical cord or circumcision.
8.Don’t give medicine to an infant younger than 2 months old
without talking to the health facility
9.Breastfeed your baby
10.If you as a new mother feel intense tired, sad, moody, or

11.Talk to your health worker if you’re worried about your

care of your baby or have food, or a safe place to live.Your
health worker can tell you about community resources or
refer you to a social worker.
12.Ensure you give your newborn six basic needs required: security, clothing, enough sleep, exclusive breastfeeding, sensory
stimulation, love, and attention
B. Safety
1.To reduce the risk of sudden infant death syndrome (SIDS):
a.Always place your baby to sleep on a firm, flat mattress
on their back, and not on the stomach or side, in a crib or
bassinet without any crib bumpers, blankets, quilts, pillows, or
plush toys.
b.Let your baby sleep in your room or crib next to your bed
until your baby’s first birthday, or for at least 6 months, when
the risk of SIDS is highest.
c.Avoid overheating by keeping the room temperature comfortable. Dress your baby for room temperature and don’t
cover your baby’s head while they’re sleeping. Watch for signs
of overheating, such as sweating or feeling hot to the touch.
d.Don’t let your baby fall asleep on a product that isn’t specifically designed for sleeping babies, such as a sitting device (like
a car seat), a feeding pillow
2.Don’t smoke or use e-cigarettes. Don’t let anyone else
smoke or vape around your baby.
3.Always put your baby in a rear-facing car seat in the back
seat. Never leave your baby alone in a car.
4.While your baby is awake, don’t leave your little one unattended, especially on high surfaces or in the bath.
5.Never shake your baby — it can cause bleeding in the
brain and even death. Call a friend, relative, or your healthcare
provider for help.
6.Avoid sun exposure by keeping your baby covered and, in
the shade, when possible.

1.2. NEWBORN WITHOUT ANY COMPLAINT- THE WELL CHILD
A well visit, or well-child visit, is a preventative health appointment with the Child’s Health caregiver with
the goal of helping well children to stay healthy in their earliest years, so they can grow into healthy adults.
The Well Child visits concentrate on the whole body, not on sickness based on the child’s age and unique
needs. The well-child visit is very important and should always include screening for health problems, growth
monitoring, and promotion.
Health education about nutrition, Immunization and protection from illness and other conditions as relevant
to the family is given. Mothers should be encouraged to take their children for visits while they are well
monthly in the first two years of life and every 3 months thereafter until 5 years old, and once a year until the
child reaches 21 years. These visits also provide opportunities to monitor mothers and talk to them about
postpartum depression or anxiety.

1.3 NEWBORN EMERGENCIES
An ill newborn can quickly become an emergency so all newborns should be treated promptly. Immediate
actions must be taken whenever these danger signs: abnormal temperature (Cold or Hot), poor sucking or
inability to suckle, breathing problem – fast breathing and severe chest in-drawing, yellowness of the hands
and body, poor feeding or not feeding at all, bleeding from the cord or redness around the cord stump,
no movement at all or only on stimulation and convulsion, are observed in a newborn. There should be
no hesitation in initiating appropriate actions or immediately refer newborn, where necessary to the next
higher-level facility.
1.3.1. DIFFICULT BREATHING
Difficult breathing means the baby has to make some effort to breathe. Rapid breathing for a newborn is
respiration rate between 40-60 per minute. Difficult breathing in children is an emergency. It may be caused
by infection, cold. In babies, chest in-drawing, flaring of nose indicate difficult breathing and a danger sign to
look out for.

1.3.3 CONDITIONS AFFECTING UMBILICAL CORD
The umbilical cord is very significant in a newborn, any infection around the area can cause Neonatal mortality.
Common indications of danger include bleeding from the umbilical cord, redness, pus, and smelly cord.These
are conditions affecting umbilical cord which could endanger the baby’s life.

1.3.12 DIARRHOEA AND VOMITING IN NEWBORN
Diarrhoea is passage of 3 or more loose/watery stools in 24 hours.
Vomiting is throwing up of contents of the stomach. Babies under six months often bring up little of their
food immediately after being fed (this is normal). A child on exclusive breast-feeding can stool for 5-8 times
daily (this is normal).
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How long has the baby had diarrhea?
2.How many times has the baby passed stool?
3.Is the baby vomiting? How often? Does the baby vomit everything?
4.Is there any other complaint such as fever?
5.Is the baby on exclusive breast feeding? If no, what has the baby
being fed with? What is the method of feeding?
6.Has any medication been given? What type?
Does the baby take breast eagerly normally or poorly?
1.General appearance- irritable, ill-looking, lethargic, unconscious, alert.
2.Head- anterior fontanelle depressed,
3.Eyes- normal, sunken, tears on crying
4.Mouth- unable to suck/drink, drinking poorly, moist tongue.
5.Chest - check respiratory rate and record.
Skin- skin pinch goes back quickly or slowly or very slowly.

1.5 ORAL HEALTH
This is care of oral cavity. Examination of the mouth of the newborn is an absolutely essential component of
examination of the child in order to detect congenital malformations, swelling, pallor, cyanosis and infections.
The areas to inspect are the lips, tongues, gum, palate, cheeks, floor and lining of the mouth.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is wrong with the baby?
2.When did you notice the problem?
3.Has the baby been ill?
4.Is the baby feeding well?
5.Was the baby born with tooth/teeth in the mouth? Or teeth
came shortly after birth?
Has the baby been given any medication?
1.General appearance- ill-looking or not
2.Mouth- roof of mouth (formed completely
3.Tongue- sore, fissured, smooth, dry, tied- check for band
holding tongue, swelling
4.Tooth- presence or absence of white patches
5.Lips- Cleft
Temperature- check and record

2.0 EARLY CHILDHOOD
The child’s first visit is a very important one and should always include screening for health problems,
growth monitoring and promotion, health education on nutrition, immunization and protection from illness
and other conditions that are relevant to child and the family. Mothers should be encouraged to bring their
children for visits while they are well monthly in the first 2 years of life, every 3 months thereafter until 5
years, and subsequent visits when necessary.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1. Birth: Were pregnancy and birth normal? Where was the child
delivered?
Did the child cry immediately after birth? Was the child jaundiced
(yellow) at any time?
2. Developmental Milestone: Is the child developing normally?
•At about the age of 2 months-sees, responds to voice
•At about the age of 4months holds head up
•At about the age of 8months sits by self
•At about the age of 12months walks alone
3.Immunizations: What immunization has the child had? Does the
child have immunization card?
4.Nutrition: How have you been feeding the child?
Exclusive breast milk if less than 6 months? Complementary
feeding, supplementary feeding and breast milk after 6 months?
What food is he/she eating now? If not exclusively breastfeeding,
is mother using any family planning method?
5.Social: With whom does the child live? Does mother work
outside the home? Who looks after the child when the mother is
away? What is your source of water? What type of toilet do you
use?
Is your house screened against mosquitoes?
Do you use Long Lasting Insecticide Net (LLIN)
6.Medical: Has the child had illnesses in the past?
E.g. Measles? Mumps? What treatment was given? Where? Any
illness or chronic cough in the family?
7. Does the child have any problem now?
Steps for physical examination
•Inspection
•Percussion
•Palpation
•Auscultation
1.General appearance: Well-looking, ill-looking
2.Colour: normal, pale, yellow, blue
3.Skin: rashes, sores, bruises
4.Head: size, swelling, colour and texture of hair, fontanelle-depressed or bulging, sutures-normal or wide
5.Eyes: discharge(sticky), yellow, red and swollen eyelids
6.Nose: discharge—blood, mucus, blockage
7.Neck: glands, masses, stiffness
8.Mouth: ability to suck normally, bleeding gums, dental caries,
pallor, sores, cracks, redness of the throat (tonsillitis), abnormality (cleft upper palate)
9.Spine: swelling or opening
10.Chest: breathing normally, respiratory rate (note if 60 per
minute or more)
11.Abdomen: condition of umbilical cord, umbilical hernia,
rigidity, tenderness, mass and distended
12.Arms and legs: normal movement, deformity, fingers and
toes (for extra or joined webbed fingers)
13.Genitalia: abnormality (check vaginal/penial opening, size
of clitoris, both testis in scrotal sac), swelling, congenital hernia.
14.Anus: Redness or prolapse, irritation and imperforation.
15.Temperature and pulse: check and record.
16.Weight: check and chart
17.Haemoglobin: estimate and record
18.Developmental Milestone
a.At about the age of 2 months — sees, responds to voice
b.At about the age of 4 months — holds head up
c.At about the age of 8 months — sits by self
At about the age of 12 months — walks alone

Mothers should be encouraged to take their children for visits while they are well—two years of life and

NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 51
Fever is elevation of body temperature above 37.50C. Fever is a sign of infection or loss of body fluid. Rapid
rise of temperature may lead to fits especially in young children of 6 months to five years (febrile convulsion).
Some common causes of fever are malaria, tuberculosis, respiratory, gastro-intestinal and other bacterial and
viral infections. All patients with a history of fever should be treated and encouraged to drink plenty of water,
other fluids and fruits. High fever should be treated as an emergency.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How long has the child had the fever?
2.Is there any convulsion?
3.Has the child ever had any convulsion?
4.Is the child having a runny nose? Cough? Or difficulty breathing?
5.Is the child coughing up blood,
6.Is the child vomiting or having diarrhoea?
7.Is the child having bloody diarrhoea?
8.Is the child having skin rashes?
9.Is the child having any ear pains? Sore throat? Headache? Stiffness of the neck?
10.Is the child passing urine normally? Is it painful? Is it more or
less frequent?
11.Is the child bleeding inside and outside the body? From the
eyes, ears, and nose?
12.Has any analgesic or other medication been given?
Is anyone currently ill in the family or compound?
Steps for physical examination
•Inspection
•Percussion
•Palpation
•Auscultation
1.General appearance: alert, restless, listless, drowsy or difficult to wake, delirious, dehydrated, thin, weak or convulsing.
2.Skin: rashes, septic spots, profuse perspiration.
3.Chest: (a) respiratory rate counted for 1 full minute.
4.Lower chest in drawing/retraction.
5.Wheeze or stridor in a calm child
6.Head: fontanelle—bulging or depressed
7.Nose: discharge–clear or purulent, blood
8.Eyes: pus, blood
9.Ear: tenderness behind the ear, discharge, blood.
10.Throat: sores, pains
11.Neck: stiffness, nodes.
12.Abdomen: tenderness, masses
Temperature and pulse: check and record
FINDING ON HISTORY
FINDINGS ON
EXAMINATION

SECTION TWO
2.4. CONVULSION
Convulsion is a jerky movement of the arms and legs and twitching of muscles. It is very common in infants
and children and can be caused by high fever from any cause, head injury, tumour or sometimes infections of
the brain or spinal cord. Muscle twitching due to tetanus or lockjaw should not be mistaken for convulsion.
Tetanus often begins with tightness of the mouth, jaw and muscle twitching.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.Has the child had a fever? Any accident? Head injury?
2.Did the child take any medicine or any substance accidentally?
3.Has the child had any previous convulsion? With or without
fever?
4.Has the child been given any medication? If yes, what?

SECTION TWO
2.5 COUGH/DIFFICULT BREATHING
Cough is usually a symptom of irritation or blockage of the air passages. It is forceful pushing of air out of the
lungs. Difficult breathing is when the child is making extra effort to breathe, is breathing too fast or having
noisy breathing. Difficult breathing can be seen by looking for chest in-drawing below the ribs and flaring of
the nose as the child breathes in. Lower chest in-drawing and fast breathing indicate pneumonia.
Fast breathing means a respiratory rate of 60 and above per minute for an infant less than 2 months of age;
respiratory rate of 50 and above per minute for an infant aged 2-12 months; and for, a respiratory rate of 40
and above per minute For a child 12 months – 5 years The most common causes of difficult breathing are
pneumonia, asthma, mucus plug, enlarged adenoids and aspiration of a foreign body or heart diseases. Aspirin
or kerosene poisoning can also cause fast breathing.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How old is the child?
2.How long has the child had the cough or difficult breathing?
3.Is there wheezing? Any previous episodes?
4.Has the child been vomiting?
5.Has the child convulsed?
6.Is there a fever? For how long?
7.Has the child had measles or immunized for measles?
8.Is the child able to drink or suck?
Steps for physical examination
•Inspection
•Percussion
•Palpation
•Auscultation
1.General Appearance: alert or abnormally sleepy or difficult
to wake, severe malnutrition, respiratory distress.
2.Nose: flaring of alae nasi
3.Chest:
a.Respiratory rate (count for 1 full minute in a calm child)
b.Subcostal retraction lower chest in-drawing
c.Stridor
d.Wheeze
4.Eyes: redness, discharge
5.Throat: soreness or redness
6.Feet: swollen
7.Temperature: check and record
8. Document oxygen saturation using a pulse oximeter if
available

SECTION TWO
2.6. DIARRHOEA
Diarrhea is the passage of three or more loose or watery stools in 24 hours. Passing 5 to 8 soft or semiliquid stools per day is normal in exclusively breast-fed infants. Children with diarrhea may quickly become
dehydrated and diarrhea in children often leads to malnutrition. The main danger in diarrheal diseases
is dehydration, and it kills children more easily than adults. Replacement of fluids is the most important
treatment. Persistent diarrhoea is one that lasts for two weeks or more. HIV/AIDS is a major cause of
persistent diarrhoea.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How long has the child had diarrhoea?
2.How many times does the child usually pass stool each day?
How many now?
3.Has the child passed any stools while in the clinic? (if so, look
at it)
4.Are the stools watery? Is there blood or mucus?
5.Is the child vomiting? How often?
6.Has the child been active?
7.Are there other complaints such as cough, fever, draining ear,
abdominal pain?
8.What has the child been fed? What is the method of feeding?
9.Has any medication been given?
10.Is anyone else in the household having diarrhoea?
Does the child drink normally, eagerly or poorly?
Steps for physical examination
•Inspection
•Percussion
•Palpation
•Auscultation
1.General appearance: alert, restless, irritable, thin, dehydrated,
lethargic, unconscious
2.Head: anterior fontanelle depressed.
3.Eyes; normal, sunken, very sunken and dry.
4.Mouth and Tongue: moist, dry, very dry
5.Chest: respiratory rate
Rapid breathing: 60 or more/minute age less than 2 months.
50 or more/minute age 2-12 months
40 or more/minute age 12 months – 5
years
6.Neck: nodes enlarged throat inflamed
7.Ears: purulent discharge, tenderness behind ear
8.Abdomen: tenderness, distended
9.Skin: skin pinch goes back quickly, slowly or very slowly
10.Weight: weigh and chart
11.Temperature: check and record
12.Offer the child something to drink, and note if the child
drinks normally, eagerly or poorly (or is not able to drink)

2.7.VOMITING
Vomiting is the throwing up of the content of the stomach. Babies under six months often bring up little of
their food immediately after being fed. This is normal. The common causes of vomiting are gastro-enteritis,
meningitis, ear infection, liver disease and abdominal problems.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How long has the child been vomiting? How often?
2.Is it associated with eating?
3.Is it projectile? Does it contain blood or greenish fluid?
4.Is there diarrhea?
5.If no diarrhea, when was the last stool? (obstruction)
6.Is the child urinating normally?
7.Does the child have any other signs of illness? Fever, cough, pain
in the head, abdominal pain?
Has the child been recently dewormed

SECTION TWO
2.8. PALLOR
Pallor is a major sign of anaemia. Anaemia is reduced number of red blood cells or reduced amount of
hemoglobin in each red cell (below 10 grams). A child can develop anaemia as a result of not eating adequate
nutritious foods, malaria, malnutrition, parasites (hook worm or whipworm), infections and sickle cell disease.
Anaemia can lead to death.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.Is there bleeding from any source?
a)Nose, gums
b)Coughing or vomiting;
c)In the stool or urine;
d)Black stools, injury;
2.Does the child have a fever? For how long?
3.Has the child had painful swelling of the fingers, toes, the back of
hands and feet? Or bone pains?
4. Does the child have a good appetite? What food does he eat?
Does the child reject some foods
5. Does the child crawl, play on the ground or go barefoot?
6. Does the child have abdominal pain?
7. Has the child been swimming or washing in the stream?
8. Does anybody in the family have similar problems?
9. Has any medication been given?
Is the mother using camphor?
FINDING ON HISTORY
FINDINGS ON
EXAMINATION
Steps for physical examination
•Inspection
•Percussion
•Palpation
•Auscultation
1.General appearance: tall and thin, malnourished
2.Chest: respiratory rate, lower chest indrawing
3.Heart: rate, murmur
4.Head: bossing of the skull
5.Eyes: jaundice or conjunctival pallor
6.Abdomen: enlarged spleen, distension
7.Limbs:
•Swollen and tender joints, feet and/or digits, or back of hands
and feet
•Nails for pallor
8.Haemoglobin: estimate and record
9.Temperature: check and record
Urine: colour, blood, protein

SECTION TWO
2.9. JAUNDICE
Jaundice is the yellow discolouration of eyes (conjunctiva) and skin due to increase bile pigment in the
body. Jaundice is as a result of the buildup of bilirubin - a reddish pigment resulting from haem metabolism
in the body. It may or may not be as a result of liver problem. The buildup of this yellow pigment may be
due to abnormalities in the formation, transportation, metabolism and excretion. At times the level may
not be clinically recognizable until it is high. Jaundice occurs in liver problem, sickle cell disease, haemolysis,
amoebiasis and tumour of the pancreas.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.Is the infant unwell? (sepsis & GIT obstruction can cause
jaundice)
2.Is there yellow discolouration of eyes and skin
3.When did you notice the yellowish discolouration of the eyes?
At what age?
4.Is there dehydration or poor weight gain? (both exacerbate
jaundice)
5.Is there family history of haemolytic disease (ABO/G6PD,
spherocytosis, sickle cell)
6.Is there dark urine or pale stools (suggest biliary obstruction)
7.What medication have you given (e.g. herbs)?
Birth trauma such as cephalhaematoma, significant bruising (breakdown of haem)

PAGE 71
Malnutrition is a state resulting from a deficiency or excess of one or more essential nutrients. Acute
malnutrition is caused by a decrease in food consumption and/or illness resulting in bilateral pitting oedema
or sudden weight loss. It is characterized by the presence of bilateral pitting oedema or wasting (low Mid
Upper Arm Circumference or low Weight for Height). Acute malnutrition comprises of both severe acute
malnutrition (SAM) and moderate acute malnutrition (MAM). All children under 5 years of age attending
clinic should be examined and have their weight charted on the growth chart. Failure to gain weight should
alert the health worker to take action.
N.B: While taking history fill the OTP (Out Patient Therapeutic Programme) card accordingly. (See Annex
for OTP Card)

Severe Acute Malnutrition (SAM)
Note: Babies who are below 6months and are malnourished are with or without medical complications are
treated at Stabilization Centres, Babies above 6 months without medical complications are treated at OTP
(Outpatient Therapeutic Programme) and Babies above 6 months with complications (hypoglycemia, high
fever, and severe anemia) are treated at the Stabilization Centre.
Correct use of RUTF
1.RUTF is a food and medicine for very thin or swollen children only. Do not share it.
2.Sick children often do not like to eat. Give small regular meals of RUTF and encourage the child to eat often
(if possible, eight meals per day).Your child should have the recommended packets per day.
3.For breastfeeding children, continue to breastfeed. Offer breast milk first before every RUTF feed.
4.RUTF is the only food sick and thin/swollen children need to recover during their time in OTP. Always give
RUTF before other family foods, such as pap, kunu, ogi, akamu, or other meals.
5.Always offer plenty of clean water or breast milk to drink while eating RUTF. Children will need to drink
more water than normal.
6.Use soap and water to wash the child’s hands and face before feeding. Keep food packets clean and well
stored.
7.Caregivers shall also use soap and water to wash their hands prior to feeding.
8.Sick children get cold quickly. Always keep the child covered and warm.
9.For children with diarrhoea, continue feeding. Give them extra food and water.
10.Return to the health facility whenever the child’s condition deteriorates or if the child is not eating
sufficiently.

SECTION TWO
2.11. HIV/AIDS
HIV/AIDS is caused by a virus, which infects human beings. AIDS is the disease condition resulting from low
immunity. Babies born to HIV-infected women may become infected during pregnancy, delivery or through
breastfeeding. At the same time, many babies who are not breastfed because of fear of HIV transmission may
die from diarrhoea, pneumonia or other diseases (not related to HIV) because they are not protected by
immune substances found in breast milk. Thus, HIV–infected mothers need to be well guided on how best
to feed their babies.
Ensure that more babies survive without becoming infected with HIV. Women can be given antiretroviral to
reduce the chances of transmitting the virus to their babies. Babies born to HIV positive women are referred
to as HIV exposed babies, a baby can only be said to be HIV positive after diagnostic confirmation.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How old is your baby?
2.What immunizations has the child taken
3.Are you breastfeeding your baby? If yes, how many times do you
breastfeed? If no, why not?
4.What are you feeding your baby with?
5.Have you introduced complementary/supplementary diet? If yes,
when?
6.How many times do you feed your baby per day?
7.How do you feed your baby?
8.Has the child been sick recently?
9.Are you on ARV?
10.Do you give your child ARV?

Infant Feeding in the Context of HIV:
Malnutrition is a common condition in HIV infected children and a major contributor to mortality in this
population. HIV infection can result in nutritional deficiencies, growth failure and developmental delay.
Malnutrition itself results in decreased immunity and greater susceptibility to infections, thus accelerating
disease progression. Malnutrition makes HIV infection worse and HIV infection worsens malnutrition.
Health Education
Advise mother/caregiver on:
1.Factors known to increase the risk of mother –to-child transmission and prevention.
2.The continuous use of ART (Nevirapine to babies)
3.Adequate nutrition.
4.Immunization.
5.Periodical deworming.
6.Regular growth monitoring.
7.Use of Long-Lasting Insecticide Nets (LLINs).
8.Personal, food and environmental hygiene.
9.The importance of attending ante-natal care (ANC) in order to provide appropriate care for the mother
and foetus.
10.Importance of taking home-based records on child’s next visit to the clinic or hospital.

SECTION TWO
2.12. MEASLES – TYPE RASH
Measles is an acute viral infection characterized by the appearance of rashes in little groups made up of
macules (flat spot) and papules (raised spots). It begins with high fever, cough, catarrh, and redness of eyes
and mouth. The mouth may later become sore and rashes appear on the whole body after the fourth day of
onset.The child may also get other serious illness like pneumonia, diarrhoea, or malnutrition at the same time
or soon after the child has had the disease. It is a serious illness because it can easily lead to complications
especially in poorly nourished children.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How long has the child been ill?
2.Does the child have a fever?
3.Does the child have cough? Is it a barking cough?
4.Is there runny nose?
5.What food is the child taking? Is the child refusing to feed?
6.Is there diarrhoea? Vomiting? (If so, may be force-feeding)
7.Has any medicine or treatment been given?
8.Are there any other children in the family under 2 years who
have not been immunized for measles?
9.Are there children in the neighbourhood with the same complaint?
General appearance: alert, abnormally sleepy, or difficult to
wake, unconscious, ill- looking or thin
1.Skin: rash, peeling, infected spots, haemorrhagic rashes
2.Chest: a). respiratory rate, lower chest indrawing
b). wheeze, stridor

SECTION TWO
2.13. SKIN DISORDERS
Skin disease is the presence of itchy or non-itchy raised lesion on the body. It may be due to poor hygiene,
allergy, malnutrition, poor housing condition as well as the environment. Some rashes can easily spread to
other members of the family therefore, early referral and correct treatment is important for the welfare
of the whole family. Some skin diseases affect the external skin (ringworm) or affect the skin internally
(pyoderma). Others affect many parts of the body systems and are generally more important (measles,
leprosy, diabetes, HIV/AIDS, Jaundice). Others are scabies, eczema, tinea vesicular, vitiligo, athletes’ foot,
urticaria and contact dermatitis.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How long has the rash been there?
2.Does it itch? Is the child scratching?
3.Are there any other symptoms? Fever, cough, headaches, difficult
breathing, sore throat?
4.Has the child eaten any unusual foods?
5.Does anyone else in the family have a similar rash or skin
problem?
6.How often do you bathe the child?
7.Where and with whom does the child sleep?
8.What medicine (including injections or home treatment) has the
child been given lately?
9.Has the child been passing excessive urine?
Has the child been having excessive thirst?

PAGE 81
Oedema is the abnormal collection of fluid in the tissues resulting in swelling of the affected part of the
body. It could be generalized or localized e.g. one foot or finger. Generalized oedema is most noticeable
on the parts of the body which are lowest at that time e.g. legs when standing. Causes include congestive
heart failure, severe anaemia, kidney disease, liver disease and kwashiorkor. Oedema may also be the result
of a reaction to toxin, drugs, and insect sting. Ascites, which is fluid in the abdominal cavity, may accompany
oedema.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How long has the child had the swelling?
2.Has the child ever had this swelling before?
3.Has the child been well otherwise?
4.Was the child bitten by an insect?
5.Has any medication been given (for example penicillin, ATS)?
6.Is there itching?
7.Is there cough?
8.Is there blood in stool or urine?
9.Any urinary frequency or pain in micturition?
Does the child eat well? What foods?
1. General appearance: pale, lethargic, puffy, distressed.
2. Chest: a) Respiratory rate, difficulty
b) Crepitations or decreased breath sounds
3. Heart: murmur, rate
4. Head: a) Hair-colour, texture
b) Face-oedema, particularly around eyes
5. Eyes: pallor, jaundice
6. Abdomen: swelling, tenderness, enlarged liver and spleen
7. Limbs: swelling, pitting oedema, thinness
8. Urine: colour, cloudiness, protein, volume, (bilharria ova in
the laboratory)
9. Weight: weigh and chart
10. Temperature: check and record
11. Haemoglobin: estimate and record
12. BP (if possible): check and record

Burns and scalds are injuries to the skin in which there is coagulative necrosis of the tissue. A burn is caused
by dry heat, for example, by an iron heat, fire, chemicals, and electricity. A scald is caused by something wet,
such as hot water, hot oil or steam. Burns and scalds can range from being a minor injury to a life-threatening
emergency. The chart below tells approximately what the proportion of a child’s total body surface is:

SECTION TWO
2.16.1 WOUNDS
A wound is any break in the continuity of the skin or mucous membrane. There may also be injury of the
underlying tissues such as tendons, large blood vessels or bones. Infections or tetanus can easily start when
the skin is broken.

2.16.2 VARIOUS BITES
Bites may be from human, animal (dogs and snakes) and insects (mosquitoes) or stings (scorpion, bees etc.).
Children are prone to bites both in the home and outside the home particularly when they go out of school.

toxoid? When?

b) Increased sweating
c) Increased respiration and pulse rate
d) patient agitated or restless
2. Bite:
a) Size and site
b) Bleeding
c) swelling, redness or warmth around bite
3. Temperature, Pulse and Respiration: check and record

SECTION TWO
2.16.3 SPRAINS, STRAINS AND FRACTURES
A sprain is a stretching or tearing of ligaments - the tough bands of fibrous tissue that connect two bones
together in the joints. The most common location for a sprain is in the ankle. Initial treatment includes rest,
ice, compression and elevation.
A strain is when a muscle is stretched too much and part of it tears. It is also called a pulled muscle.
A fracture is a sudden break in the continuity of a bone causing deformity, pain, tenderness and loss of power
or movement.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How did the injury occur? When?
2.Was there any bleeding?
3.Where is the pain? How bad is it?
4.Can the child move/use limb? Is there limitation to movement?
Which movement causes greatest pain?
1.General appearance:
a) Signs of shock-increased sweating, pallor, restlessness
b) Degrees of pain
2. Limb or Joint
a) Tenderness or swelling
b) Warmouths or redness
c)Crepitus (crackling)
d) Open wound
3. Temperature: Pulse and Respiration: check and record

SECTION TWO
2.17. DISORDERS AFFECTING THE HEAD
The head contains the brain (with the organs for the sense of hearing, seeing, smelling and tasting). The
bones of the head are very strong but sometimes a serious head injury may be caused by a fall or a blow to
the head. Some signs of serious head injury are bleeding from the nose, mouth or ears, severe headache or
unconsciousness. There also may be vomiting or the client may be confused. Any delay in referring a client
with a head injury for proper medical treatment may cause permanent injury or death.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is the problem?
2.Has he fallen or hit his head? When?
3.If he has had an injury, has he been drowsy?
4.Has he vomited? How many times?
5.Does he have a headache?
6.Has he convulsed?
Is he playing and behaving normally for his age as he usually does?
1.General appearance and behaviour: dull, abnormally, sleepy,
or difficult to wake, restless, delirious or unconscious
2.Head: a) Circumference,, shape
b)External injuries, sores, or rashes
c)Swellings or depressions
3.Neck: stiffness
4. Eyes: haemorrahage, pupils size (equal or unequal), reaction
to light, vision, jaundice
5.Gait: steady or unsteady
Temperature, Pulse and Respiration: check and record.7. Haemoglobin: check and record

SECTION TWO
2.18 NECK DISORDERS
Neck problems include the presence of enlarged nodes and lumps around the neck. There could also be
pain or stiffness of the neck. These are usually caused by infection of the ears, the mouth or on the scalp,
tuberculosis, leukemia (cancer of the blood), meningitis, and other childhood malignancies.There may also be
an injury to the neck following a fall.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is the problem?
2.How long has the child had the problem?
3.Is there weight loss or loss of appetite?
4.Is there fever or sore throat?
5.Is there excessive sweating at night?
6.Is there headache? Earache?
7.Is there pain or vomiting?
8.Is the child restless or hyperactive?
Has the child convulsed?

3.Mouth: a). condition of teeth

5.Neck: stiffness, nodes, lumps, glands

SECTION TWO
2.19. EYE DISORDERS
Eye problems are disease conditions affecting the child’s eye and are commonly caused by bacteria or viruses,
injury or foreign body or lack of enough vitamin A in foods. If these conditions are not prevented or treated
early, they may cause blindness. It is therefore important to prevent or treat eye infections.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is the matter with the child’s eye?
2.Do you think the child sees?
3.How long has the child had the problem?
4.Has the child had it before?
5.Did the problem come suddenly or gradually?
6.Is there a lot of pain in the eye?
7.Does the child rub the eye frequently?
8.Did anything enter the eye?
9.Has any medication been applied to the eye? If so, what?
10.Does anyone else in the family have this problem?
11.Is the child receiving treatment for any other disease?
Does the child take palm oil, carrots, fresh mangoes, pawpaw, and
tomatoes?

SECTION TWO
2.20. EAR, NOSE AND THROAT DISORDER
Ear Problem
Ear problems, such as middle ear infections, can be detected early by observing common symptoms like
ear pain, dullness or redness of the ear drum, and sometimes, perforation and drainage of pus or fluid. It’s
crucial to examine the bone behind the ear, as tenderness could indicate mastoiditis, a potential complication.
Another complication is deafness, which can be tested by clapping your hands behind the child to see if they
respond. Early detection empowers healthcare workers and parents to take proactive steps for their child’s
health.
Nose Disorders
The nose, an organ for breathing and smell, can be affected by diseases or injuries in the throat or sinuses.
This can lead to symptoms like watery discharge, mucous blood or pus from the nose. Healthcare workers
play a vital role in identifying these issues, especially if there’s discharge from only one side of the nose, which
could indicate a foreign object. This highlights their value and integral role in the healthcare process.
Throat Disorders
A red throat, often a sign of infection, may be combined with nasal congestion and middle ear infection,
especially in young children. It’s important to note that tonsils normally enlarge during childhood. However,
unless they show signs of difficulty breathing or inflammation, no treatment is needed. This reassurance can
give parents a sense of relief and confidence in their child’s health.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is wrong with the child?
2.Does the child have hearing problems?
3.Is there any discharge from the ear?
4.Has the child had any ear surgery?
5.Is there any family history of deafness?
6.Does the child have runny nose?
7.Is there any bleeding from the nose? If yes, for how long?
8.Has the child had it before?
9.Is the child able to swallow food or fluids?
10.Has the child received any injection or medication recently? If
yes, what is the name? Ototoxic drugs (antibiotics (e.g gentamicin), diuretics, and cytotoxics).
11.Has the child been exposed to loud noises recently? Exposure
to noise (e.g. pneumatic drill or shooting)
What treatment have you given to the child for this condition?

SECTION TWO
2.21. ORAL HEALTH DISORDERS
Oral health is a state of being free from chronic mouth and facial pain, oral and throat cancer, oral sores, birth
defects such as cleft lip and palate, periodontal (gum) disease, tooth decay and tooth loss, and other diseases
and disorders that affect the oral cavity.
The most common complaint is pain which may or may not be accompanied with swelling inside the mouth
or on the face. The condition of the mouth is also a reflection of the client’s general health, so it should
be inspected as part of physical examination. Early recognition of oral problems with prompt attention or
referral will reduce morbidity and mortality arising from otherwise simple disease condition.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is the problem?
2.Where is the problem?
3.Is there pain from tongue, palate, mucus membrane, teeth or
gum?
4.How long has it been noticed or present?
5.Are you able to eat your regular diet?
6.Does the pain increase with hot or cold drink or food?
7.Does the pain start on its own?
8.Does it keep you awake at night?
9.Is there swelling anywhere?
10.Is there any bleeding from gum or other part of the mouth?
11.Do you notice bad or foul breathe?
12.What do you use for cleaning your teeth?
13.How do you clean your teeth?
Do you notice any sore in the mouth?
1.General appearance: ill looking, facial swelling, malnourished.
2.Temperature: Check and record.
3.Mouth:
a)Gum: sore, swelling, discharging sinus, bleeding, white
patches.
b)Tongue sore, coated, redness, fissures, smoothness, dryness.
c)Lips: crack
d)Chest: lining
4.Teeth: caries, discolouration, tenderness, mobility, missing,
plague.

Health Education
Advise Mother/caregiver on:
1. Ensure brushing twice daily, in the morning and before bed at night.
2. Use fluoride-containing toothpaste for effective dental care.
3. Replace the toothbrush as soon as the bristles begin to flare or scatter.
4. Schedule regular dental visits at least twice a year for routine cleaning, scaling, and polishing.
5. Avoid consuming sugary foods between meals and opt for mouth-cleansing fruits instead.
6. Maintain a diet rich in adequate nutrition for overall oral health.
7. Cultivate the habit of rinsing the mouth with water after meals.
8. Discourage harmful traditional practices such as uvulectomy.
9. Emphasize the importance of maintaining home-based records during visits to the clinic/hospital.

SECTION TWO
2.22. CHEST/HEART DISORDERS
A very rapid, slow, or irregular heart rate is always a serious sign. Changes in pulse and respiratory rate are
often important signs of heart disease. Blueness of the lips or fingers, clubbing of the fingers and toes with
difficult breathing are signs that the heart is not able to do its work well or easily. Congestive heart failure
occurs when the heart becomes too weak to pump all the blood around and some of the fluid goes back into
the lungs and often into the abdomen and legs. Any history of shortness of breath should make the health
worker think of a heart disease.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is the problem? For how long?
2.Is there cough?
3.Is there difficulty with feeding due to shortness of breath?
4.Is there shortness of breath at night?
5.Is there chest pain?
6.Is there restlessness? (sign of lack of oxygen)
7.Does the child tire easily or tend to squat much of the time?
8.Have the lips or fingernails ever looked blue?
9.Does the child participate in sports? Is the child able to run
around with his/her age mates without difficulty?
10.Any other problem?
1.General appearance: ill-looking, weak, thin, pale
2.Eyes: swollen or puffy
3.Nose: flaring
4.Mouth: blueness of lip
5.Toes/fingers: thickness of toes/fingers, blueness of the fingers
6.Chest: respiratory rate, lower chest in-drawing, grunting
7.Heart: rate and rhythm, murmur
8.Assess child’s heart rate:
Normal Heart rate

4-8years - 90/min

10.Temperature: check and record

2.23. ABDOMINAL DISORDERS
The common causes of abdominal pain are gastro-enteritis, malaria, appendicitis, rupture of the intestine,
typhoid, sickle cell crisis and obstruction, e.g. strangulated inguinal and umbilical hernias. Constipation, worm
infestation may also cause abdominal pain. Abdominal pain may lead to hardness or rigidity of the abdominal
wall.
HISTORY

7.Is there constipation?
8.Has the child passed worms in the stool?
9.Are stools black or is there bright red blood in stools?

2.25. SEXUAL ASSAULT/ RAPE
Sexual assault is rape or any sexual contact that results from coercion, including seduction of a child through
offers of affection or bribe; it is also includes being touched, grabbed, kissed or shown genitals. On the
other hand, rape is oral, anal or vaginal penetration that involves threats or force against a person who is
unwilling (i.e. non-consenting) or incapacitated (because of cognitive or physical disability or intoxication).
Such penetration, whether wanted or not is statutory rape if victims are younger than the age of consent.
This may result in pregnancy (if female), transmission of STIs (e.g. HIV, Syphilis, depression, physical injuries,
recurrent dreams and nightmares and other emotional problems. Victims of sexual assault/rape should be
advised to report the incident to police if not against their wish.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.Was the baby sexually assaulted (raped)? If yes, where? How?
Who did it?
2.How many times? How long did it last? When?
3.Was the baby threatened or forced?
4.Was the child made to drink anything (alcohol or medicine)
prior to the assault?
5.Was the baby wearing clothes when the incident occurred?
6.Was the child physically harmed?
7.Was there any penetration of the anus, vagina or mouth? If yes,
with penis, finger or object?
8.Was there ejaculation? Where?
9.Any bleeding? Where?
10.Did you bath, change clothes or clean up the child afterwards
11.Has the child been sexually assaulted before?
12.Did you report to anyone else about this? Who?
13.Have you given any treatment? When? Where?
14.Any witness? Any other problem?
1.General appearance: restless, physically exhausted, conscious,
depressed, afraid, crying.
2.Check temperature, pulse, respiration, blood pressure and
record.
3.Mouth: injuries
4.Skin: injuries
5.Genitalia: bleeding, injuries
6.Anus: injuries
7.VDRL: test and record.

2.26. ANAL DISODERS
The anus is the terminal end of the alimentary canal. Common problem affecting the anus include fissures.
Redness and prolapsed.An anal fissure is painful li near s or eat margin of anus. In rectal prolapsed; the rectum
turns inside out and protrudes out of the anus as a pink mass. It is common in young children who are poorly
nourished, have worms and diarrhoea frequently.

2.27. WORM INFESTATION
Worm infection is the presence of large amount of worm in the gut which causes discomfort to the patient.
Most children have worms.Ascaris (round worm) is the commonest. Heavy round worm infestation produces
abdominal pain, vomiting, constipation and sometimes obstruction of the intestine. Hookworm infestation
is more common in rural areas where heavy infestation causes blood in stool, anaemia, malnutrition. Severe
(whip worm) infestation causes bloody diarrhoea, abdominal pain, anaemia and prolapse of the rectum.
Thread worms also live in the intestine and cause itching around the anus. Tapeworm is a flat ribbon - like
worm that can also be found in the intestine.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is the problem? When did it start?
2.Has any worms been passed? If yes, describe
3.Is there diarrhoea? Blood in stool?
4.Is there vomiting? Is the child coughing? Any wheezing?
5.Is there anal itching?
6.Is there abdominal pain and rectal prolapse?
7.Is there any other problem?
Has any medication been given? If yes, what?
1.General appearance: thin, pale,
2.Eyes: pallor
3.Chest: wheezing, rhonchi
4.Abdomen: tenderness, distension, visible peristalsis
5.Anus: redness, prolapserectum
6.Temperature Pulse and Respiration: check and record
7.Haemoglobin: estimate and record
8.Weight: weigh and chart
Stool examination: for ova of worm (if laboratory available)

NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 117
A urinary tract infection, commonly known as UTI is one of the most common urinary problems and is
an infection that occurs in the urinary tract. This infection is most often caused by microbes such as fungi,
bacteria, and virus and involves the kidneys, ureters, urethra, or bladder. This condition can be very painful,
and the consequences can get critical if the infection spreads to the kidneys. Children may present with
abnormalities of the genitalia such as undescended testes, imperforate hymen, hydrocele, hypospadias, and
hermaphrodite.
Children may also present with painful micturition with or without discharge. Redness or itching of the
genitalia may occur in genital infections. Patients may complain of discharge, pain and burning sensation on
passing urine and blood in urine may occur as a result of schistosomiasis infection. Other causes of urinary
problems are kidney disease and diabetes which can cause frequent micturition.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is the problem?
2.How long has it existed?
3.Is there pain on passing urine? Or does the child scream
or rub his/legs together before passing urine? If yes, for how
long?
4.When last did he urinate?
5.What does the urine look like? Odour?
6.Has the child been bed wetting after a period of full bladder
training?
7.Do ants cloud around urine passed by the child?
Is the child excessively thirsty?
1. General appearance: puffy, pale, ill -looking
2. Eye: pallor, peri -orbital oedema
3. Mouth: dryness, parched lips
4. Abdomen: tenderness, or masses, oedema
5. Flanks: tap both flank with hand for tenderness
6. Skin: dryness, wrinkle
7. Limbs: hands and feet for oedema, clubbing
8. External genitalia: abnormalities, redness, swelling or pus. In
males, look at foreskin to see if is too tight or will not retract.
9. Temperature pulse and respiration: check and record
10. Urine; cloudiness, colour, odour, protein, blood sugar, and
microscopy,

SECTION TWO
2.29. ARM AND LEG DISORDERS
Deformities of the arms and legs, if not corrected early could lead to permanent disabilities. The deformities
or swellings may be due to bruises, fractures or a dislocation. It is important to test for normal movement
of the parts whenever there is a history of injury, swelling or deformity. Swelling of a joint may be due to a
sprain of the tendons surrounding the joint. Sickle cell disease may also be a cause of pain in the arms, legs
and the hip bone.Tuberculosis can occur in the joints. Polio can seriously affect one or both legs or arms first
causing weakness then wasting, or paralysis. Diabetes Mellitus may cause tingling sensation and numbness of
the fingers and toes, foot ulcer, that fail to heal and black discoloration of toes and feet (Gangrene).

Is there fever?
Is the child a sickler?

SECTION TWO
2.30. CHANGES IN ALERTNESS AND BEHAVIOUR
Changes in alertness and behaviour mean that the client does not act normal. He may be drowsy or
unconscious or give a history of fainting. Big changes such as unconsciousness or delirium are easy to identify.
Small changes such as drowsiness and decreased alertness are not easily noticed. If the client is not alert or
is drowsy, it may not be noticed easily. If this happens, the client may not receive treatment and could come
back later in more serious conditions. Therefore, careful observation of client is important.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How long has the child been like this?
2.Did anything happen to the child? Any injury?
3.Did the child have a fit or convulsion, or is the child clumsy?
4.Has any medication been given?
5.Has the child been ill otherwise?
6.Has the child had fever, diarrhoea, vomiting or ear pain?
7.Has the child been drinking more recently???
8.Has the child been urinating more frequently?
9.Was there any difficulty with the pregnancy or delivery?
10.Was the child jaundiced at birth or shortly after?
Has the child been frothing in the mouth or passing urine when
he faints?
1.General appearance: (especially for alertness and irritability),
sign of trauma.
2.Chest: respiratory rate, lower chest in-drawing.
3.Neck: stiffness
4.Head: fontanelle for bulging, signs of injury.
5.Ears: pus, redness or perforation of the drum.
6.Limbs: arms and legs for abnormal movements.
Temperature: check and record
8.Haemoglobin: estimate and record.
Urine: test for protein, sugar, acetone.

SECTION TWO
2.31. POISONING
A poison is a toxic substance which may enter the body through swallowing, breathing, absorption through
the skin or mucous membrane or infection. Poison may be accidental, suicidal, or homicidal. An overdose of
a medicinal drug may act as poison e.g. overdose of aspirin. It is common for children under 5 years of age
to accidentally ingest medicine not prescribed for them or over dosage of prescribed, drugs; alcohol (gin),
kerosene, petrol or corrosive substances e.g. acid, lead or bleaches.
Poisoning can be harmful effects caused by the ingestion, inhalation, absorption, or injection of a toxic
substance into the body. Poisons can come in various forms, including chemicals, gases, plants, medications,
household products, and venom from certain animals or insects.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is the problem?
2.What was the substance taken? How much of it? When was the
poison taken?
3.Has any medication been given (modern or traditional)?
Has the child vomited? Is there blood in the vomitus?

2.General appearance: consciousness, alertness, pallor, cyanosis.
3.Eye: size and reaction of pupils to light.
4.Chest: respiratory rate, difficulty, lower chest indrawing,
deep and sighing.
5. Abdomen : tenderness, rigidity.
6.Mouth: burn, bleeding, dribbling, colour of membrane
7.Pulse: check rate, volume and record.
Temperature: check and record.

2.32. EMERGENCY CONDITIONS
In children 2 months to 5 years can be life threatening and should be treated promptly to save life of the
child. There should be no hesitation in asking for or referring where necessary. Severe acute fever, diarrhoea,
malnutrition, child poisoning. conjunctivitis, epilepsy, choking, anaphylaxis are emergency situation and should
be treated as soon as possible to save the life of the child.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1. What is the problem with child?
2. Is there fever, vomiting, diarrhoea or abdominal pain?

4. Is the history of poisoning?
5. Is the child having difficulty in breathing or chocking?
6. Is there family history of asthma?
7. Is a child allergic to known substances or food?
8. Is there decrease in urine output?
1. General appearance: ill looking, alert, unconscious, convulsing, dehydrated.
2. Temperature: check and record
3. Eye: Sunken
4. Mouth and lips: Dryness
5. Chest:
(a) Respiratory rate: check and record
(b) Wheezing, crepitation
6. Skin: dryness, dehydration

SECTION TWO
2.33. HIGH RISK GROUPS (ORPHANS AND VULNERABLE CHILDREN)
An orphan is a child (0 – 17 years) who has lost one or both parents. A child is vulnerable if, because of
the circumstances of birth or immediate environment, is prone to abuse or deprivation of basic needs, care
and protection and thus disadvantaged relative to his or her peers. (FMW &SD 2008). A vulnerable child is
one: with inadequate access to education, health and other social support, has a chronically ill parent, lives
in a household with terminally or chronically ill parent(s) or caregiver(s), live outside of family care (lives
Overview with extended family, in institution, or on the street), is infected with HIV in addition to the above,
a vulnerable child includes, children in need of alternative family care, children who are sexually abused or
neglected, children in hard-to-reach areas; children with mental disability; children affected by armed conflict
and children in need of legal protection. Most of the current interventions cover OVC aged 6-17 years,
leaving out children under five years old.
The general appearance include Growth failure, Signs of malnutrition, Poor personal hygiene, Evidence of
poor health care e.g. delayed immunisation, untreated infection etc., Frequent injuries from lack of supervision
Behavioural indicators include Self-simulating behaviours (biting, sucking), Infancy lack of social smile and
stranger anxiety, Antisocial behaviour (destructiveness, stealing, cruelty to animals or people), Extremes of
behaviour (aggressiveness, demanding, over complaints), Suicide attempts., Lags in emotional and intellectual
development (especially language).Psychoneurotic reactions (hysteria, obsession, compulsions, phobia, etc.).
Apart from the government at various levels, a number of organizations are involved in OVC work in Nigeria.
They include international NGOs, mainly USG and Global Fund implementing partners, local NGOs, FBOs
and CBOs. Some of the main international NGOs involved in OVC work include the Columbia University
International Centre for AIDs Care and Treatment Programs (CU-ICAP), Catholic Relief Services (CRS),
Centre for Development and Population Activities (CEDPA), and Winrock international AIM.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1. How old is the child? Who is the child leaving with?
2. Is the child sick or well?
3. If sick, what kind of illness?
4. If well, who is taking care of the child?
5. Is the mother or father a live both or one
6. Is the mother or both parent HIV/AIDs before death?
7. Has the child been sexually abused and is sick as result of that?
1.General appearance: well-nourished, ill, tired, clean, thin,

SECTION TWO
2.34 SCHOOL HEALTH SERVICES
The school health services are essential in maintaining and improving the health status of member of the
school community and should be available to everyone for as long as they are in the school community.
School health services afford the opportunity for personal health care, screening of school food vendor,
environmental health care, community mobilization, Advocacy. Personal health care includes health screening
for all newcomers, regular health inspection and treatment for minor ailments.

3.0 MIDDLE CHILDHOOD
SECTION THREE
3.1 CHILD FIRST VISIT
This section is for children 6 - 12 years. The health worker should be able to carry out simple sight and
hearing tests and to be able to find out whether or not the child has visual or hearing problems that can
affect school performance. He must also recognize social problems likely to put the child at risk, must be able
to screen for malnutrition. He/she should request for genotype and blood group so that the child/parents
know the blood group and genotype as well as counsel if abnormality is detected. He should carry out health
and nutrition education; immunize and protect him/her from illnesses and other conditions as relevant to the
family. Encourage mother/guardian to take their children for clinic visit even though they are well.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.Development; Ask Parent/caregiver if child is developing normally.
2.When does the child start crawling, walking, and speaking?
3.Is the child attending school?
4.What is the current class of the child? (Milestone)
5.Family and Social;
a)With whom does the child live?
b)Do Parent /caregiver work outside home?

3.2 FEVER
SECTION THREE
Fever is the elevation of the body temperature. It is body temperature of 37.50C or above. Fever is a sign
of infection or loss of body fluid. Rapid rise of temperature leads to fits (Convulsion) especially in young
children. Some common causes of fever in children are malaria, tuberculosis, respiratory, gastrointestinal
and other bacterial infections and viral infections. All patients with history of fever should be treated and
encouraged to drink plenty of water, other fluids and fruits, and should not be overdressed. High fever should
be treated as emergency.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How long has the child had the fever? Is there any convulsion?
2.Has the child ever had any convulsion?
3.Is the child having a runny nose? Cough? Or difficult breathing?
4.Is the child coughing up blood?
5.Is the child vomiting or having diarrhea?
6.Is the child having bloody diarrhea,
7.Is the child having skin rashes
8.Is the child having any ear pains? Sore throat? Headache? Stiffness of the neck?
9.Is the child passing urine normally? Is it painful?
10.Is it more or less frequent?
11.Is the child bleeding inside and outside the body. from the eyes,
ears, and nose
12.Has any analgesic or other medication been given?
Is anyone currently ill in the family or compound?
1.General appearance: alert, restless, listless, drowsy or difficult to wake, delirious, dehydrated, thin, weak or convulsing.
2.Skin: rashes, septic spots, profuse perspiration.
3.Chest:
(a)Respiratory rate counted for 1 full minute.
(b)Lower chest indrawing/retraction.
4.wheeze or stridor in a calm child Nose: discharge – clear or
purulent., blood Eyes: pus, blood
5.Ear: tenderness behind the ear, discharge, blood.
6.Throat: sores, pains
7.Neck: stiffness, nodes. Abdomen: tenderness, masses
8.Temperature and pulse: check and record
9..

SECTION THREE
Convulsion is jerky movements of the arms and legs and twitching of the muscles. It may be caused by injury
and infection of the meninges, brain and spinal cord. Tetanus or lockjaw must not be mistaken for convulsion.
It begins with tightness of mouth, jaw and muscle twitching.

NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
3.4 COUGH
SECTION THREE
Cough is a sign of irritation of the throat and wind pipe which comes suddenly. In children, common causes
of cough include pneumonia, asthma, mucus blocked nostrils, adenoids, aspiration of foreign body or poison
e.g. kerosene. Heart diseases may also present with cough.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS

the child been vomiting?

able to drink?
Has any medication been administered to the child? If so, what
medication?

to wake, severe malnutrition, respiratory distress.

3.5 DIARRHOEA
SECTION THREE
Diarrhoea is characterized by the passage of watery stool more than 3 times a day. In children, it stands
as one of the leading causes of morbidity and mortality, posing a greater risk than in adults. Hence, fluid
replacement stands as the cornerstone of treatment. Causes of diarrhea encompass dysentery, typhoid,
cholera, ingestion of poisons, contaminated foods, and HIV/AIDS. Persistent diarrhoea refers to a condition
lasting two or more weeks.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How long have you been ill?
2.How many stools do you usually pass each day? Now?
3.Have you passed any since coming to the clinic?
4.Are the stools watery? Is there blood or mucus?
5.What is your source of drinking water?
6.What type of toilet facilities do you use?
7.Are you nauseated or have you vomitted?
8.Do you have fever? Abdominal pain? Cough?
9.Are you sometimes constipated?
10.Is there anyone else in the household who is sick?
11.Have you taken any medication or treatment for this?
Have you eaten plenty of fresh fruits (e.g. mangoes) or plenty of
pepper?
General appearance: alert, restless, irritable, thin, dehydrated,
lethargic, unconscious
Eyes; normal, sunken, very sunken and dry.
Mouth and Tongue: moist, dry, very dry
Chest: respiratory rate
Neck: nodes enlarged, throat inflamed
Ears: purulent discharge, tenderness behind ear
Abdomen: tenderness, distended
Skin: skin pinch goes back quickly, slowly or very slowly
Weight: weigh and chart
Temperature: check and record
Offer the child something to drink, and note if the child drinks
normally, eagerly or poorly (or is not able to drink)
SEE APPENDIX ====for chart on assessment of dehydration

3.6 VOMITING
SECTION THREE
Vomiting is the throwing up of the content of the stomach. The common causes of vomiting are gastroenteritis, typhoid, cholera, meningitis, ear infection, liver disease and abdominal problems. A child who is
vomiting should not be given large or heavy foods but small frequent and freshly prepared food. Children
vomiting should be cared for urgently through fluid replacement.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.How long have you been vomiting?
2.How many times a day do you vomit?
3.What is the vomitus like? Projectile (think of obstruction),
greenish? With blood?
4.Is there diarrhoea? If not, when was the last stool?
5.Do you have abdominal pain?
6.Do you have any cough, fever or headache?
7.When was your last menstrual period? (if female)
8.What was your last meal?
What medication have the child taken?
1.General appearance: ill looking jaundiced, dehydrated,
distressed.
2.Mouth: tongue coated, clean.
3.Throat: redness, imflammed tonsils
4.Neck: stiffness.
5.Abdomen: tenderness, rigidity, visible peristalsis, distended.
6.Ear: redness, bulging or perforation of drum, discharge
7.Hydration status: eyes, tongue, mouth, skin
8.Temperature and pulse: check and record
Weight: Check and record

Headache is a common complaint which may be a symptom of many diseases such as: Malaria, sinus infection,
high blood pressure, ear, nose and throat problems, nervous tension, meningitis, brain abscess and typhoid
fever. It can come on when there is constipation or inadequate sleep and rest.

Pallor is a major sign of anaemia. Anaemia is reduced number of red blood cells or reduced amount of
hemoglobin in each red cell (below 10 grams). A child may develop anaemia as a result of not eating adequate
nutritious foods, malaria, malnutrition, parasites (hook worm or whipworm), infections and sickle cell disease.
Anaemia can lead to death.

SECTION THREE
Jaundice is yellowish discoloration of the conjunctiva and skin due to an increase in bile pigmentation. It is
commonly due to excessive breakdown of red blood cells (haemolysis) as in sickle cell crisis, severe malaria
and liver problems e.g infective hepatitis, obstruction of the bile duct, etc. It is these conditions rather than
the Jaundice itself which demands treatment.
PAGE 148
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
3.10 NUTRITIONAL DISORDERS
SECTION THREE
Nutritional disorder is a condition caused by improper diet between what an individual eats and what he
requires to maintain health. This can result from eating too much or excess or an inadequate diet such as
protein, fat and carbohydrate.
Children with nutritional disorders have higher risk of developing other diseases, and death. Identifying
children with nutritional l disorders and treating them can help prevent many severe diseases and deaths.
Stunting is common among this age group especially children from families with low income in rural
communities and urban slums.
HISTORY
