PAGE 305
MANAGEMENT OF NORMAL LABOUR
First Stage of Labour:
• Explain the condition to the client.
• Explain all procedures and seek permission for examination.
• Ensure and respect the privacy of the woman.
• Discuss findings with the client and keep her informed about the progress of labour.
• Encourage active movement alternating with rest between contractions and frequent emptying of the
bladder.
• Encourage the woman to bathe or wash herself and her genitals at the onset of labour.
• Ensure infection prevention.
• Avoid vaginal examination during contractions.
• Do not shave the perineal area.
• Do not administer an enema during labour.
• Start a Labour Care Guide when labour is in the active phase.
• Allow the client to choose a more comfortable position.
Second Stage of Labour:
• Continue to monitor maternal and fetal vital signs.
• Swab the vulva with a mild antiseptic lotion.
• Discourage bearing down until the head is at the perineum (teach the woman to breathe through the
• Ensure controlled delivery of the head by taking the following precautions:
• Keep a hand on the head as it advances with contractions.
• Cover the anus with a perineal pad using your other hand.
• Observe the perineum for signs of tear.
• Ask the mother to breathe deeply and steadily with her mouth open and not to push during the delivery
of the head.
• Feel gently around the neck for the presence of the cord; if the cord is loosely felt, deliver the baby through
the loop, but when it is tight, clamp and cut it, then unwind it from the neck.
• Allow for natural restitution.
• Deliver the anterior shoulder by applying gentle pressure downward.
• Lift the baby upward toward the abdomen to deliver the posterior shoulder and the rest of the body.
• Place the baby on the mother’s abdomen.
• Note the time of delivery.
• Tie/Clamp The Cord in 2 Places and Cut the Center After About 2-3 Minutes.
• Provide immediate newborn care (Refer to Newborn Section).
• Avoid the following:
• Rectal examination.
• Lithotomy position with or without stirrups.
• Fundal pressure during labour.
SECTION SIX
mouth to prevent pushing before the head has distended to the perineum).
PAGE 306
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
• Verbal abuse.
Third Stage of Labour (Use Active Management of Third Stage of Labour - AMSTL):
• Palpate the abdomen to rule out the presence of another baby.
• Administer a uterotonic IM oxytocin 20mg on the thigh within one minute after birth or IM or IV Carbetocin
100ug stat OR Tabs Misoprostol 600ug (3 tabs) under the tongue (sublingual).
Perform Controlled Cord Traction (CCT) as follows:
• Clamp the cord close to the perineum.
• Use the other hand to apply counter traction on the uterus, just above the pubic bone.
• Use a forward and downward motion to deliver the placenta.
• Maintain steady but gentle pressure.
• Cup the placenta in your hands and deliver it gently once it is visible at the perineum to ensure that the
membranes are intact.
• Examine the placenta and membranes to ensure completeness.
• Clean the mother and check the perineum for laceration or bruising.
Fourth Stage of Labour:
• Massage the uterus every 15 minutes for the next 2 hours to ensure that it is well contracted.
• Continue to check vital signs.
• Ensure breastfeeding is initiated to promote bonding.
• Monitor the amount of bleeding.
• Observe for any danger signs in both mother and newborn.
• Teach the mother about perineal care.
SECTION SIX
Best Practices:
• Close monitoring during the first 6 hours.
• Monitor BP, Pulse, Vaginal bleeding, and uterine hardness; every 15 minutes for 2 hours, every 30 minutes
for 1 hour, and every hour for the last 3 hours.

6.3 POSTNATAL DISORDERS
SECTION SIX
The postnatal period spans 6-8 weeks after delivery. During this time, lactation becomes well established,
and the uterus and other reproductive organs begin to return to their pre-pregnancy state. It is advisable
to encourage at least three postnatal visits for check-ups to assess the well-being of both the mother and
baby. These visits also provide a crucial opportunity to screen postnatal mothers for signs of mental health
conditions such as puerperal psychosis, involvement in substance use/abuse, and signs of domestic violence,
enabling appropriate management and referral when necessary.

6.4 FAMILY PLANNING
SECTION SIX
Family Planning is the process of assisting clients in achieving their desired number of children and determining
the timing of pregnancies, or avoiding conception altogether. While oral contraceptive pills are a common
method, some women may encounter difficulties in consistently taking them, leading to potential adverse
effects.These issues can range from mild to severe, requiring the health worker to provide follow-up care and
take appropriate action. Additionally, family planning consultations present an opportunity to screen mothers
for signs of mental health conditions such as anxiety, depression, substance use/abuse, and domestic violence.
Identifying these issues allows for their proper management and referral to relevant services.
HISTORY

6.5 INFERTILITY
Infertility is defined as the inability of a couple to conceive after one year of regular unprotected intercourse.
Male infertility may arise from various factors, including deficiencies in sperm production, blockage of the
sperm delivery system, testicular injury, hormonal disorders, or poor testicular descent. Similarly, women
may encounter challenges in conceiving, which could stem from factors such as infections, lack of awareness
regarding fertile periods, or irregular menstrual cycles. Mental health conditions like anxiety, depression,
substance use/abuse, and domestic violence can also impact a couple’s ability to conceive.
However, community health practitioners are equipped to provide appropriate advice and take necessary
actions to address infertility issues. Moreover, infertility consultations present an opportunity to screen
mothers for signs of mental health conditions, substance use/abuse, and domestic violence, facilitating their
proper management and referral when needed.
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
Menstrual History: When was her last menstrual period (LMP)
Gynaecological History: Any signs of infection, vaginal discharge,
lower abdominal pain, pain or bleeding on intercourse? How was
the infection treated? Any operation D & C. Caesarian section.
Abortion? Where and when was it treated? Any problems after it
such as bleeding, discharge, fever, pain.
Obstetric History: Has she ever been pregnant? With this man?
Any problems after the pregnancy - bleeding, discharge fever,
lower abdominal pain? What happened to the pregnancy? How
long ago was it?
Contraception History: What method of birth control has she
used?
When did she stop? Any problems with the method - bleeding,
discharge, fever, lower abdominal pain, no menstrual period?
Sexual History: How often do they have intercourse? Does her
husband have other wives? Does her husband have children? Does
her husband live with her? How long have they been trying? Any
sexual problem – pain on intercourse? Sexual history is important
in her husband.
Medical History: How old is she? How does she feel now? Has
she come to the clinic or seen the doctor in the last 6 months?
For Husband; Has the husband had mumps before? Any history of
injury to the genitals? Any undescended testicles?
Social History: Any history of alcohol use? Any history of substance use/abuse? Any history of domestic violence? Any history
of mental problem?
General appearance: tired or pale check skin for signs of
domestic violence.

poor physical health or small vaginal opening.
Abdomen: scars, masses.

size of the uterus, masses, tenderness of the uterus or adnexa.

6.6 PRE-MENOPAUSE
Pre-menopause is a natural process marking the transition to the menopausal stage, characterized by the
cessation of a woman’s menstrual periods due to advancing age, signifying the conclusion of her reproductive
years. This phase entails fluctuations in the menstrual cycle, accompanied by various physical and emotional
symptoms, all attributed to hormonal changes within the body. Pre-menopause typically spans between 2 to
10 years before the onset of menopause.
HISTORY

7.0 GERIATRIC HEALTH
The World Health Organization (WHO) defines the elderly as people whose age is 60 years and above.
It’s important to note that there are a significant and growing number of elderly persons in the population.
This means that the PHC worker can expect an increasing number of elderly patients. This group of patients
(clients) are vulnerable and at risk of having chronic diseases such as hypertension, diabetes, osteomyelitis,
cataracts, nyctalopia, hearing impairment, dementia, nutrition and social problems. They spent most of their
lives in nation-building, either in the public or in the private sector, when they were much younger. They
deserve the best of care from the country they served.
There are differences in the signs and symptoms of diseases presented in the elderly and young people.
Therefore, diagnosis in the elderly may require more precise history/laboratory tests. Due to some changes
associated with the ageing process, such as difficulty in hearing, speech problems, memory loss, etc., it would
be necessary for a family member, who plays a crucial role in the care of the elderly, to be involved in their
care. An understanding of the needs of the elderly is essential for PHC workers, as is the recognition of the
invaluable support that family members can provide in this process.
7.1. MUSCULO - SKELETAL SYSTEM DISORDERS
SECTION SEVEN
As adults age, they experience a gradual loss of minerals and living materials from their bones, resulting in
weaker and more brittle bones. This physiological process increases the likelihood of fractures and broken
bones among the elderly. Additionally, osteoarthritis, a condition associated with aging, can cause pain and
difficulty in normal joint movement.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS

7.Do you have joint pain or stiffness?
8.Do you have back pain?
9.Do you have fever?
10.Are you on any medication?
1.General appearance: look out for distress
2.Temperature: check and record
3.Limbs: check for deformity, swelling on the affected part,
localized severe tenderness/pain, inability to move part

7.2. CARDIOVASCULAR SYSTEM DISORDERS
SECTION SEVEN
Cardiovascular problems are conditions affecting the heart and blood vessels. When these vital components
function optimally, blood flows smoothly throughout the body. However, disruptions in the circulatory system
can occur due to either poor heart function or blockages in blood vessels, leading to inadequate blood flow
through arteries and veins and potentially resulting in serious health complications.
While some individuals are born with cardiovascular issues, most develop over years. Consequently, there
is a higher prevalence of cardiovascular diseases among the elderly population. Common conditions such
as arteriosclerosis, atherosclerosis, heart attack, heart failure, and stroke are prevalent in older adults. High
blood pressure is a major contributor to these diseases and is more prevalent in the elderly compared to
younger individuals. Other contributing factors include high blood sugar or diabetes, high cholesterol, and
dyslipidemia.
Fortunately, early detection and appropriate management of these risk factors can often prevent death from
cardiovascular diseases. Therefore, it is crucial to identify and treat these risk factors promptly and refer
individuals to appropriate healthcare providers for further management.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is the problem?
2.How long have you had it?
3.Do you have cough, chest pain, or shortness of breath?
4.If cough is present, do you bring out sputum? Colour?
5.And you sleep flat or do you need several pillows to sleep?
6.Do you sometimes have to get up at night struggling to breathe?
7.Have you ever lost consciousness?
8.Any sudden fall?
9.Do you have difficulty in moving any part of the body?
10.Do have any history of high blood pressure or diabetes?
11.Are you on any medication?
General Appearance: look out for respiratory distress
Temperature: check and record
Cardiovascular System (CVS).
a. Pulse: check for rate, rhythm, volume b. Blood pressure:
check and record
c. Heart sound: check for heart sounds, murmur
Chest: check for respiratory rate, signs of dyspnea
Abdomen: check for distension, tenderness, enlarged liver
Central Nervous System (CNS): check for level of consciousness and paralysis
Lower limbs: check for pedal swelling/oedema
Urine – Urinalysis – protein, glucose, etc
Blood – Haemoglobin determination
Eye – Visual acuity
Ears – Test for hearing
Weight – weigh and record (BMI)

Drugs that can be used in management of cardiovascular system
problem in primary health settings;
1. Antihypertensives: Used to
control high blood pressure.
Examples include ACE inhibitors
(e.g., lisinopril), beta-blockers (e.g.
metoprolol), and diuretics (e.g.
hydrochlorothiazide).
2.Antiplatelet Agents: Help prevent
blood clots. Aspirin and clopidogrel are examples.
3. Statins: Used to lower cholesterol levels and reduce the risk of
cardiovascular events. Atorvastatin
and simvastatin are commonly
prescribed.
4. Beta-blockers: Besides hypertension, they may be used for conditions like angina and heart failure.
Examples include metoprolol and
carvedilol.
5. Angiotensin II Receptor Blockers
(ARBs): Similar to ACE inhibitors,
they help manage blood pressure
and may be prescribed for heart
failure. Losartan is an example.
6. Calcium Channel Blockers:
Used for hypertension and certain
heart conditions. Amlodipine and
diltiazem are examples.
7. Antiarrhythmics: Prescribed
for irregular heart rhythms.
Amiodarone and flecainide are
examples.

Health Education
1.Counsel client on the need to have adequate rest and go to bed early.
2.Advise to avoid mental and emotional stress.
3.Encourage moderate exercise e.g-walking.

6.Encourage Intake of fluids, and vegetables
7.Encourage intake of complex carbohydrate whole gain e.g sorghum, wheat, guinea corn.
8.Encourage keeping personal records of BP and blood sugar.
9.Avoid self-medication.
10.Advise adherence to medication, clinic, and laboratory appointments.

7.3 GENITO- URINARY DISORDERS
Genito-urinary problems are prevalent among the elderly and can stem from various factors such as
metabolic changes, age-related alterations, infections, and abnormal growths. Examples include diabetes,
vaginal or uterine prolapse, enlarged prostate, cancers, and schistosomiasis.
Patients may present with symptoms such as discharge, pain, and a burning sensation during urination
(dysuria), blood in the urine, frequent urination, and urinary retention, among others
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is the problem?
2.How long have you had it?
3.Do you urinate very often? How often? Night and day?
4.Do you have difficulty in passing urine or pain/burning sensation
when passing urine)?
5.Do you have feeling of incomplete passage of urine?
6.Are you able to control urine?
7.Do you drink more than usual?
8.Do you have pain in the flank?
9.Is there blood in your urine?
10.Do you have fever?
11.Do you have a history of wadding in streams in childhood?
(Schistosomiasis)
12.Have you had this problem before? Have you had any form of
treatment? If yes, which?
13.Do you have any vaginal discharge or itching (if male, any
discharge from the penis?)
14.Any history of weight loss
15.Are you on any mediation?

7.4 GASTROINTESTINAL DISORDERS
Gastrointestinal issues affecting the elderly may arise from various causes including infections, ulceration, and
malignant changes. These conditions can manifest as constipation, cholera, diarrhea, gastritis, peptic ulcer, or
cancer of the colon, stomach, or esophagus.

in a day?

intermittent?
9.Have you passed any worms in stool?
10.Do you have fever?

7.5 SPECIFIC PROBLEMS OF THE ELDERLY
Elderly individuals commonly experience age-related issues such as hearing and vision difficulties, along with
complaints like coughing and high blood pressure. Additionally, conditions such as heart attack, osteoarthritis,
arthritis, and peptic ulcers can arise due to heart or respiratory problems. Irregular meals are also a concern
for many elderly individuals, as they may lack the strength to prepare their own meals, leading to malnutrition
in some cases. Moreover, diabetes is prevalent among the elderly population.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is the problem?
2.How long have you had the problem?
3.Is there pain in the eye/ear?
4.Did you (or anyone) put anything into the eye/ear? If yes, what?
5.Do you have any chronic disease such as high blood pressure or
diabetes?
6.Is there any family history of blindness, deafness, high blood
pressure or diabetes?
7.Do you observe ants around your urine?
8.Do you eat food regularly? Any change in appetite?
1.General appearance :look out for distress,
2.Temperature: check and record

4.Eyes: check for:
5.Pallor
6.White patch in both pupils (cataract)
7.Visual acuity using Snellen’s chart
8.Ear: check for wax
9.Chest: check for difficulty in breathing

7.6 MENTAL DISORDERS
The risk of developing mental health disorders rises as individuals’ age. The World Health Organization
(WHO) estimates that mental health issues affect over 20% of the elderly population. Despite this significant
prevalence, mental health problems often go unrecognized among older adults. The most common mental
health disorders in this age group are dementia and depression, with others including anxiety disorders,
substance use problems, and self-harm. Additionally, elderly individuals are vulnerable to neglect and
maltreatment by family members, which can exacerbate or contribute to the development of mental health
challenges.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is the problem?
2.For how long has this problem been there? Has it happened
before? If yes, when?
3.Is there persistent tiredness
4.Is there persistent sadness or anxiety?
5.Is there loss of interest or pleasure in activities that used to be
interesting and enjoyable?
6.Is there loss of appetite, nausea?
7.Is there headache and/or fever?
8.Is there difficulty in carrying out usual work or daily task?
9.Are there any problems with sleeping or waking up?
10.Is there frequent complaint of multiple symptoms with no
obvious physical cause?
Is there decline or problems with remembering things (memory loss) and orientation (loss of awareness of time, place and
person)?
Is there loss of emotional control (easily upset, irritable or
crying)?
Is there any family history of similar problems?
1.General appearance: look out for state of personal hygiene, ,
irritability, smell of alcohol or tobacco, signs of injury
2.Temperature: check and record
3. Blood pressure: measure and record
4. Eyes: check for pallor
5. CNS: check for – ability to remember (memory)
awareness of time, place and person (orientation)

7.7 ORAL DISORDERS
Oral health encompasses freedom from chronic mouth and facial pain, oral and throat cancer, oral sores,
birth defects like cleft lip and palate, periodontal (gum) disease, tooth decay, tooth loss, and other disorders
affecting the oral cavity.
Among the elderly, oral health issues are common physiological changes, often evidenced by high levels of
tooth loss, dental caries, and periodontal diseases. Early recognition of oral problems, coupled with prompt
attention or referral, can significantly reduce morbidity and mortality arising from otherwise manageable
conditions.
HISTORY
EXAMINATION/ACTIONS
1.What is the problem?
2.Where is the problem?
3.Is there pain in the teeth or gum?
4.When was it noticed?
5.Are you able to eat your regular diet?
6.Does the pain increase with hot or cold drink or food?
7.Does the pain keep you awake at night?
8.Is there bleeding from gum or any other part of the mouth?
9.What do you use for cleaning your mouth?
10.How do you clean your teeth? How many times a day?
General appearance: ill-looking, malnourished, facial swelling,
Mouth:
Gum: look out for sores, swellings, discharging sinuses, bleeding and white patches.
Tongue: look out for sore, coating, redness, dryness, c. Lips:
look out for cracks
Teeth: Look out for caries, discoloration, shaky or missing
teeth, plaque.
Temperature: Check and record

CLINICAL

ACTION

NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
8.0 HEALTH FACILITY MANAGEMENT
INTRODUCTION / PREAMBLE
The National Health Policy (1988) formally embraced Primary Health Care (PHC) as the cornerstone and
entry point to health for all Nigerians, aligning with the national philosophy of social justice and equity. It
is a vital component of the country’s health system, with a central role and primary focus, contributing
significantly to the overall social and economic development of the community. Primary Health Care is not
just a strategy for achieving universal coverage, but also a philosophy that involves members of the political
wards in which the Primary Health Centre is located, in management and decision-making for the health
facility in their location.
Over the years, the Primary Health Care system has not only received increasing government support
through policy reviews, improved funding, and implementation frameworks, but it has also demonstrated its
potential for success.These interventions, such as the Primary Health Care Under One Roof (PHCUOR) and
the Basic Health Care Provision Fund (BHCPF), have been instrumental in revitalizing and improving PHC
service delivery, painting a promising picture for the future of healthcare in Nigeria.
Given the current global health crisis and the need to optimize PHC service delivery in a post-COVID era,
it is crucial to prioritize the training and development of Officers-in-Charge (the OICs) of Primary Health
Centre facilities. These individuals, who are at the forefront of our platform for achieving the Sustainable
Development Goals (SDGs) and Universal Health Coverage (UHC), must be equipped with the right skills
and tools for effective management. This is not just a recommendation, but a necessity for the future of
healthcare in Nigeria.
It is widely acknowledged that the majority of Primary Health Centres across the country are led by
health personnel in the cadre of Community Health Extension Workers (CHEWs), Community Health
Officers (CHOs), and Community Nurse/Midwife Officers. Their capacity in managerial skills is a critical
factor for organizational success and improving it will have a positive impact on service delivery at the
PHC facilities. This section is dedicated to enhancing the managerial capacities of these Community Health
Practitioners, empowering them to effectively manage these healthcare facilities and deliver quality care to
the populace. The key management activities, derived from various operational guidelines, manuals, and job
aids developed by the National Primary Health Care Development Agency (NPHCDA), are designed to
provide basic tools for governance, financial management, infrastructural maintenance, quality improvement,
reporting, monitoring, supervision, and mentoring tasks at the PHC level.
8.1
HEALTHCARE FACILITY MANAGEMENT
8.1.1
Concepts, Definitions, Principles and Functions of Management.
SECTION EIGHT
Healthcare Facility Management:
There are many definitions for different purposes. Perhaps the shortest is that management is “getting things
done.”
The principle underlying this definition is “commitment to achievement,” i.e., commitment to purposeful
action, not action for its own sake.To stress this notion of purpose, the definition may be rewritten as follows:
“Management is saying what one wants to be done and then getting it done.” In other words, management
ensures that objectives are specified (i.e., specifically what is to be achieved) and then that they are achieved.
Health facility management coordinates facilities, resources, and people to provide the best possible patient
care. The goal of managing a healthcare facility is to enable the highest standard of care, whether the facility
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 343
is a large hospital with 1,000 beds or a small health post. The goal is to enable caregiving at the highest level
by ensuring the facility runs smoothly.
The Facility Management Committee (FMC) is a crucial component of all Healthcare Facilities. It is responsible
for ensuring the smooth operation of the facility and comprises the following key members;
•Officer In-charge (OIC) of health facility.
• Heads of PHC Departments.
• A representative of WDC/VDC, as necessary.
During their regular monthly meetings, the Ward Development Committee (WDC) and Village Development
Committee (VDC) should, depending on the situation, receive updates and feedback from the Facility
Management Committee (FMC).
Two committees provide support for the WHS:
The Ward Development Committee (WDC) – operates at the ward level, and
The Village Development Committee (VDC) – operates in urban and rural community settings.
Principles of Management
Management by Objectives:
A management principle implies that one should decide and say what is to be accomplished by setting an
objective (a goal, a purpose, an end, a target). Objectives should state what is to be done, how much is to be
done, where it is to be done, when it is to be completed and the standard by which it will be possible to tell
whether or the extent to which it has been achieved. Setting objectives helps those who are to produce the
result to organize, distribute and coordinate their efforts. It helps determine when the intended result is being
achieved or has been achieved. In other words, a clear statement of objectives is essential for effectiveness.
Effectiveness is not just a measure of how well an objective has been achieved; it is a constant goal that
management strives to improve. For instance, if a health team in PHC, Cana sets the objective of immunizing
100 children against diphtheria by providing Penta vaccinations during the following week, and succeeds in
immunizing 95, the work has been managed effectively. However, if an epidemic of diphtheria were to occur a
few weeks later, how would you judge the team’s performance? The urgency and importance of effectiveness
is further highlighted when considering a team objective like holding daily clinics from 9.00 – 5.00pm, and
providing 24/7 services. If, by the end of 2022, the children under 5 of Cana have not been fully immunized,
and the objective was for 85% to be fully immunized, it might seem very likely that those in charge did not
manage their programme effectively. If the health centre at Cana remains closed for two days during the
week ending 27 July, can it be said that the objective of holding daily clinics has been achieved effectively? This
constant evaluation and striving for effectiveness is what drives continuous improvement in management.
Learning from Experience
How is this principle applied? When there is a gap between objectives and results (or achievement),
management analyzes why only the observed results were achieved and why they fell short of the set
objectives. Some causes can be easily remedied, and action is taken accordingly. Others cannot be removed
SECTION EIGHT
The management principle that underlies the comparison of objectives with their achievement in order to
judge effectiveness is known as ‘learning from experience’. In the context of management, ‘learning from
experience’ refers to the process of reflecting on past actions and outcomes, and using these insights to
inform future decision-making and improve performance. It derives from and justifies - proves the value of the first management principle, namely, management by objectives.
PAGE 344
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
in the short term and are called constraints.
Management learns from this process and uses what it has learned to achieve its objectives. This process is
sometimes called ‘feedback’ (of information from experience to decision for action).
A second reason for specifying objectives is that it enables management and health workers to decide how to
achieve them. In other words, stating the objective, or end, helps select the means of achieving it. Sometimes,
however, it needs to be clarified which ends and which means.
When pipe-borne water has been installed, when a health worker has been trained as a skilled birth attendant,
and when a guide for primary healthcare management has been produced, are these ends or means?
When people have accepted the use of latrines when children have been immunized and when health workers
evaluate their effectiveness, can we talk of ends or means?
The answer is, of course, both. Every end may be seen as a means to a higher end, almost indefinitely: trained
staff is a means of delivering better services; better services, in turn, are a means of improving a community’s
health; better health is a means of ensuring a better quality of life.
The following definition of management mentions both ends and means:
Management is: “Getting things done through people.”
This does not mean that someone orders and others do what they are ordered to do, although this is often
what happens. It means that people are the most important means, or resource, for getting things done.
Getting things done through people means that people must work, performing certain activities and tasks to
reach certain ends or objectives. When we are concerned with how means are used to reach ends, we are
concerned with efficiency.
Efficiency is about reaching ends by only the necessary means or the least wasteful means. It measures
the relation between the results obtained and the effort expended (by the health team, for example). This
concept has many implications for resource use.
SECTION EIGHT
Almost any work involves more than one person.When two or more people are involved in work or activity,
management must apply two complementary principles: division of labour and convergence of work.
Division of Labour:
When work is divided, or distributed, among group members, and the work is directed and coordinated, the
group becomes a team. In a team, and generally, when there is specialization and division of labour, with each
category of staff exercising its skills towards achieving the objectives, management entails assigning a balanced
proportion of each kind of staff to the work to be done.
Efficiency implies that all necessary means (but no more than necessary) are used to achieve objectives and
that the least expensive is chosen if there is a choice of equally effective means. The team approach is how
management attempts to bring about balance among the different members of the team and the work they
do. For instance,
•For a surgeon to operate, someone must give the anaesthetic.
•Someone must lay out the instruments.
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 345
•Someone must ensure that the instruments and the theatre clothes have been sterilized.
• Electricians must ensure the power supply, and other staff must be responsible for other tasks.
The general principle of management illustrated by this example is division of labour: work must be shared
by, or divided among, several categories of technically skilled people. As pointed out earlier, one of the
advantages of defining objectives and considering resources is that this helps. In deciding how to reach the
objectives, i.e., the activities needed to achieve them and the methods and organization that will enable
people to work harmoniously towards the objectives while using resources efficiently. The study of work
activities and the organization of work relations form a significant area of management. Its governing principle
is that of convergence of work.
Convergence of work
Convergence of work means that the activities of the various people who do the work come together to
achieve objectives. The activities should be designed, assigned and directed to support each other in moving
towards a common goal. It also implies that working relations - how the team members interact with one
another- should contribute to the success of each activity and, thus, to general effectiveness. In general, health
activities are studied, described, and performed under three main headings, namely:
•Service activities
•Development activities
• Support activities
A service activity (e.g. immunization) usually requires some preceding development activity (e.g. training of
immunizers) and some continuous support activity (e.g. provision of supplies). These three kinds of activity
need to be managed to bring about convergence of work, balance of resources and harmonious work relations,
and ultimately, the intended results. Specific work activities must be brought into logical relations with one
another. For instance, trained staff is needed to provide services. Specific objectives are set for training staff.
To achieve these objectives, various preliminary development activities are carried out in relation to one
another, e.g., designing a curriculum, preparing learning materials, selecting trainees, and recruiting trainers.
Activities must also have a time (or temporal) relation or sequence. For instance, trainers can be recruited
first and asked to prepare the curriculum and the learning materials, then select trainees and begin teaching.
However, other time sequences are possible. Also, training can begin either before staff recruitment (before
beginning to provide the services) or after staff recruitment, when training is given ‘on-the-job’, i.e. at the
same time as services are provided.
There is also a place (or spatial) relation between activities. For instance, Services are given in families’ homes
(e.g. treatment of illness); on-the-job training may take place elsewhere in the district; formal training is given
in a classroom at the health centre or a training college; the curriculum may be designed in the capital city.
Management is: “the efficient use of resources”
It is well recognized in health work, as in other kinds of activity, that the different types of resources used for
achieving objectives must be carefully balanced. Use of the team approach to bring about balance among the
SECTION EIGHT
Frequency is an aspect of the time relationship. In the work of a health team, some activities (such as certain
development activities) occur only once or rarely; some (such as services) occur more often, and some (such
as support) occur continually.
PAGE 346
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
different people (human resources) concerned has already been mentioned. However, other resources must
also be managed to achieve objectives; examples are materials (equipment and supplies) and money (without
which people and materials often would not be available). Beyond these ‘three Ms’ - manpower, materials,
and money – there are time and information, which the health team must also have. These other types of
resource must also be kept in balance. For instance:
If there is insufficient equipment (e.g. syringes) or a shortage of supplies (e.g. petrol), a health team may not
be able to reach its immunization target.
When money runs short, salaries cannot be paid on time and staff morale drops, and this may result in targets
not being reached.
A prime concern of management is therefore to ensure adequate funds and stocks of materials (commodities,
diagnostic supplies and drugs) to permit the staff to provide the planned services. This is one meaning of
“efficient use of resources” in the above definition of management.
If all resources were plentiful and free, the concept of efficiency might have no practical importance. However,
some - often many - resources are scarce and costly. For instance:
Wazobia State has an ample supply of trained people to staff the health services. But the local authorities are
poor and their budget does not allow them to fill vacancies without cutting down on supplies, such as petrol
for transport or essential drugs (both of which are imported and therefore costly).
This is a typically difficult position for management, which illustrates one more concept - economy - and one
more principle - substitution of resources.
In everyday life some commodities become scarce, either because production or supply falls or because
demand for them rises. As a result, they become more expensive. When a commodity becomes scarce or
more expensive, ordinary people buy less of it; in other words, they economize.The concept of economy - or
careful use of resources - is applied by almost everyone in daily life, with more or less success. The related
principle, substitution of resources, is also widely applied.
Often, when the resources normally used to provide services become scarce or too expensive, different
resources or a different balance of resources may be used to produce the intended results. For instance:
When purchasing food for a hospital kitchen, cheap produce in season can be substituted for other similar
but more expensive food if it is equally nourishing.
SECTION EIGHT
When petrol continues to go up in price, bicycles may be substituted, at least partly, for motorized transport.
The savings on petrol, vehicle maintenance and drivers may be better spent on more field staff (perhaps
saving time previously lost on travel), thus achieving the same coverage of the population as before.This leads
to the fifth principle of management, substitution of resources.
Substitution of resources:
Labour substitution, a pivotal resource management strategy in healthcare, is the utilisation of trained
auxiliary personnel or volunteers for tasks previously handled by professionals. This practice, similar to the
use of generic drugs in health management, is a potent tool for cost reduction without sacrificing quality,
particularly in countries where generic drugs are dependable alternatives to brand-name preparations.
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 347
One more definition may be considered briefly, which brings together many of the points discussed so far:
Management is:“getting people to work harmoniously together and to make efficient use of resources
to achieve objectives.”
This definition underscores the vital role of teamwork in healthcare management. The working relations
between individuals, often referred to as functional and structural, are a primary focus for managers. Functional
relations are directly derived from the technical aspects of the work, including where, when, and in what
sequence it is performed. An example is the working relationship between the members of a surgical team.
Structural relations, on the other hand, are related to administrative rules and standards, and particularly to
the authority and responsibility assigned to individuals - for instance, who hires and fires staff, how much a
medical officer may spend without consulting higher authorities, or to whom the public health nurse must
submit her reports and accounts.
The two key concepts that underlie functional and structural relations within a health team are responsibility
and authority, which can hardly be considered separately in day-to-day teamwork management. For instance:
A member of the staff of a health centre who is entrusted with the responsibility for the immunisation
coverage of the community must also be given the authority to requisition vaccines from the central cold
storage depot, to order transport from the vehicle pool for the immunisation team; to have the refrigerator
repaired if necessary; to replace leaky syringes and used needles with new materials from the stores. This
empowerment ensures that the immunisation programme will be carried out effectively. This stress on the
importance of responsibility and authority should empower healthcare managers in their decision-making
roles.
An officer responsible for “directing and supervising” staff but with no say in their leave, transfer, or promotion
would be unable to fulfil his or her responsibilities.
The management principle derived directly from the need to associate responsibility and authority may be
stated as functions determine structure.
Management is: “to make decisions”
This definition is more general than those given earlier. It stresses the most essential element of management,
namely decision-making. A decision is a choice between two or more courses of action. It can be a resolve
to act - “this shall be done” - or, sometimes, not to act - “this shall go on as before”. Some decisions deal
with quantity- “more of this, less of that, none of the other”; some deal with quality- “higher coverage, better
services”. In management terms, a decision is an answer to a question about possible courses of action, an
answer that can be stated simply as yes, no, more, or none. “I don’t know” in this context is indecision.
The authority of a health team member can be defined as the decisions that that member is authorised to
make. A common management issue is the lack of clear responsibility for urgently needed decisions or a
situation where someone is responsible but lacks the necessary authority to act. For instance:
SECTION EIGHT
Functions determine structure:
When work is clearly defined, i.e., the function and duties of individual team members are clearly defined and
known to all, and the working relations (the structure) follow.
However, what is the exact nature of authority? One more definition of management will help clarify this
point:
PAGE 348
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
The ambulance has broken down in the bush.The driver, upon securing police watch over the vehicle, reports
to his officer-in-charge, who is “responsible for the maintenance and upkeep of transport”. However, the
breakdown truck belongs to the State Primary Health Care Development Board, and the charge for picking
up the ambulance will likely be more than, say, N25,000.The officer-in-charge has the authority to spend up to
N10,000 on repairs, and the Local Health Officer up to N15,000 only and LGHA has already spent its budget
allotment for transport. The questions are: Who is the decision-maker, and where is he or she?
A primary concern in working relations is enabling the most suitable person to make decisions where and
when necessary. Any health team member may be called upon to decide at one time or another, especially
when the team leader is absent; otherwise, the team would be paralysed. A health worker who strictly
follows the rules may do nothing and is then blamed for doing nothing, say in an emergency. What is needed,
then, is the application of the principle of delegation.
Delegation:
Delegation occurs when someone with authority ‘lends’ the authority to another person, conditionally or
not, to enable that person to take responsibility when needed. This management principle has been stated
with a humorous twist: “Never do yourself what another can do for you as well as you would.” This advice is
for the busy manager, but it could also apply to anybody’s family life and the performance of the tasks of any
member of a health team. Information and decision rules are needed to decide between different courses of
action. For instance:
A management decision for Health Team X is whether to transfer the responsibility for managing labour and
delivery from one staff category (A, which could be nurses) to another (B, which could be midwives). The
relevant decision rule might be: “If managing deliveries takes more than 20% of A’s work time, and if B, after
training, can conduct ten consecutive deliveries without fault, then A may authorize B to take over this task”.
Information is needed to show that the deliveries take over 20% of A’s time, that B has been trained, and that
B has passed the test.
Another crucial element of decision-making is ensuring that the decision, once taken, is effectively
communicated to all concerned. This is not just about sharing information; it’s about making everyone feel
valued and integral to the process. Many people would even say that all concerned should know beforehand
that a decision is about to be made. Often, knowledge about the need for a decision, and taking part in making
it, are the best assurances that the decision will subsequently be acted upon. One thing is essential, however,
and that is communication between those who make decisions, those who implement them, and the people
affected by the decisions. At least, the decision itself should be communicated, but normally the supporting
information, the decision rules, and how the decision is to be implemented, should also be included.
Management by exception:
SECTION EIGHT
Management by exception means two things:
First: be selective. Do not become overloaded with routine and unnecessary information. Keep your mind
available for critical information, on which you will be required to act. For instance: If you were in charge of
fifty beds, you would not want to know every day every patient’s blood pressure, temperature, pulse rate,
respiration rate, bowel movements, volume of fluids passed, etc. Just one of these elements of information
might be all you would need to watch in each case to manage most patients effectively and efficiently. This
is the principle of management by exception, which is particularly relevant in a healthcare setting where
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 349
managers need to focus on critical patient information rather than being overwhelmed by routine data.
Second: Make big decisions first.To be overloaded with petty decisions (such as scheduling staff breaks) may
result in more important ones (such as implementing a new patient care protocol) being neglected, or what
has been called “postponing decisions until they become unnecessary”. In short, management by exception
means selectivity in information and priority in decision.
Shortest decision-path:
The last management principle discussed in this session, the principle of the shortest decision path, deals
with the issue of who should make which decision. (and often when and where, as well). Answering this type
of question will help clarify the delegation of authority referred to earlier. Applying this principle means that
decisions are made as close as possible in time and place to the object of the decision and those affected by
it. This saves time and work (e.g., in transmitting information) and also ensures that decisions can take full
account of the circumstances that make the decisions necessary and in which they are put into effect.
The main functions of management:
A function may be defined as a group of activities with a common purpose.
Management is not just a set of tasks; it’s a series of functions that are crucial to the success of a health team.
A health team, in particular, has three main management functions: planning, implementation, and evaluation.
These functions are not just about getting things done, they are about inspiring and motivating the team to
achieve their goals.
Thus, the principle of management by objectives requires the specification of what and how much is to
be done, and where and when it is to be done. Each of these four questions needs one or more planning
decisions. For instance, a planning decision could be deciding the number of staff needed for a particular shift.
The sum of the planning decisions constitutes the planning function of management in a health team.
The principle of delegation is concerned with authority and responsibility, i.e., with functional relations
between people working together to achieve some purpose. The types of decisions involved are concerned
with the organization of working relations to ensure effective and efficient work, i.e., implementation.
Organizational decisions are part of the implementation function of management.
SECTION EIGHT
Applying the principle of learning from experience requires the analysis of gaps between desired results and
actual results or achievement and the use of the information obtained from the analysis in decision-making.
This is, in other words, a measurement and a judgement of performance, or the evaluation function of
management, a function that contributes significantly to the success of a health team.
PAGE 350
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
8.1.2. LEADING AND MANAGING HEALTHCARE FACILITY
I. Distinction between Leaders and Managers:
Leaders: Have a vision of what can be achieved, then communicate this to others and evolve strategies for
realising the vision.They motivate people and can negotiate for resources and other support to achieve their
goals.
Managers: Ensure the available resources are well organised and applied to produce the best results. In
resource-constrained and complex environments, a manager must also be a leader to achieve optimum
results.
Attributes of a good leader should include:
• A sense of mission.
• Be charismatic.
• Ability to influence people to work together for a common cause.
• Be decisive.
• Ability to use creative problem-solving to promote better care and a positive working environment.
b)Attributes of a manager should include:
• Clarity of purpose and tasks.
• Good organisational skills.
• Ability to communicate tasks and expected results effectively.
• Ability to negotiate various administrative and regulatory processes
• Good delegation skills
I. Conditions for Good Management:
Certain conditions are essential for creating good management, including:
• Managers and team members need to be selected on merit
• Managers need to earn the respect of their staff, patients, and supervisors
• Managers need to have the knowledge, skills and understanding of the role, tasks and purpose of the
services they deliver.
• Basic support systems function well; clear staff administration rules and regulations; well-planned and
timely delivered supplies, equipment, and drugs; clear and transparent financial processes; and well-planned
and monitored activities.
SECTION EIGHT
II. Leadership and Management Roles of the Officer-In-Charge (OIC):
The Officer-In-Charge of the PHC Health Centre is responsible for providing leadership and managing all
activities in the PHC. The OIC performs four significant functions, namely;
• Planning
• Organising
• Leading
• Controlling
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 351
Planning Functions of the OIC
Planning in healthcare management is a systematic process that involves identifying future goals, outlining
appropriate actions, and determining the resources needed to achieve them. It is a decision-making process
that considers past and current circumstances. Planning is the foundation of all management functions and
precedes all others.
The OIC may be engaged in the following types of planning:
a) Strategic planning: a dynamic and deliberate process that allows the PHC to invent the future, usually in
about 3-5 years. This deals more with outcomes and impacts
b) Operational planning is establishing plans that relate to running a PHC on a short-term basis. It deals
more with services, outputs and setting targets. This is the most critical part of the PHC’s running and is
supposed to be led by OIC in conjunction with the WDC and FHC.
The significant steps in planning are as follows:
Step 1. Problem identification: Define the present situation of the PHC (situational analysis) by providing
answers to the following questions
1. Where are we now?
a. Geographic coverage
b. Target population
2. What is the magnitude of the problem?
3. How visible is it?
4. Is it new or old?
5. Are there effective interventions
Step 2. Stakeholder’s analysis and analysing strengths and weaknesses (SWOT) of the PHC: This is
applied by examining the following:
1. Strengths: What are the strengths of the PHC? What do we do well?
2. Weaknesses: What are the weaknesses of the PHC? What do we need to improve?
3. Opportunities: What are the external trends that offer opportunities?
4. Threats: What are the local, State and National threats that pose risks?
Appropriate: to goals and strategies
Realistic: Given the limited amount of resources
Time-Bound: What is the date by which the change will occur? This will help with resource allocation and
activity planning.
Step 4. Develop action plan (road map): The action plan describes what to do, who is to do it, how to do it,
when, and the outputs/deliverables. A sample action plan is in Annex VI.
SECTION EIGHT
Step 3. Establish goals and objectives: Always make objectives SMART:
Specific: What is the specific change desired? Who is the target group? What is the location?
Measurable: What is the measure (number or percentage) to monitor progress/performance?
PAGE 352
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
Step 5. Develop budgets: After developing activities to be executed, cost them to determine the resources
needed to implement the plan. Any plan that is not costly is not practical. The Account Officer and other unit
heads will be beneficial in determining unit costs and cost elements of particular activities. At this point, it is
also important to identify where the required resources will likely come from.     
Step 6. Implement plans: The OIC should ensure that the plans are implemented. The OIC is responsible
that resources are available to implement the plan. He should also liaise with relevant stakeholders to ensure
it happens.
Step 7. Monitor plans:While the heads of units are responsible for monitoring the plans of their various units,
the OIC is also responsible for monitoring the overall plan. The OICs are encouraged to extract monthly
plans from the annual plans and display them in conspicuous places in their offices. The use of pictograms
and charts is encouraged to make this happen. Equally, all the Heads of the Unit and OIC are advised to
have a weekly and daily to-do list. The weekly management meetings and any other meetings also allow for
monitoring of the plans.
Organising Functions of the OIC
This function refers to how the OIC distribute resources, delegates tasks, structures departments, sets
staffing levels, etc.This function encompasses everything from assigning right fit tasks to the appropriate team
members to deciding how those team members relate to each other in an organisational structure.
The OIC should ensure that all staff members are aware of the organisational structure of the PHC and the
reporting relationships. Despite being in different units, there is a need to work harmoniously to achieve
the objectives of the health facility, which is to provide quality healthcare services that meet the needs of
the people they serve. Orientation is important for any new staff in the facility. Where the staff available are
short in supply of requirements and task-shifting and task sharing are to be practiced in line with the relevant
policies, there should be constant meeting with the staff to explain why this is necessary and clarify roles fully.
The checklist should also be provided to staff for any new task assigned to provide appropriate guidance on
proceeding with such tasks until the staff masters the processes involved.
Organising the different units within the health facility to ensure effective workflow processes and patient
flow to reduce the stress patients go through is critical. For instance, the facility records section, which is the
first place of contact with the client, should be conspicuous and easily accessible. The waiting section should
also be spacious to accommodate clients (based on service demand projections) almost always.There should
also be a designated place where patients could stay when there is an overflow, especially on ante-natal days.
SECTION EIGHT
Leading Functions of the OIC
Leadership is a process by which the OIC influences others (other medical staff, patients, community and
stakeholders) to accomplish the required activities and outcomes. Leading is not about what one can achieve
on their own; it is about inspiring people to be the best they can be at what they do.
In leading, the OIC must be able to;
Effectively plan and manage work and resources, lead people, and guide future leaders or people who can
take over from him.
To effectively plan and manage work and resources, the OIC must;
•Plan work to be done
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 353
• Maximise resources (both human and material)
• Take decisions with the information available on issues like procurement, maintenance, and
• Staffing and staff deployment
• Be flexible enough to change direction if need be
To lead the other staff members, the OIC should:
• Provide clear guidance
• Build a team
• Acknowledge the teammates
• Provide feedback, and above all
• Set good examples.
To guide the future leaders, the OIC should:
• Hold people accountable, including him/herself
• Delegate to individuals and teams
• Coach individuals and teams
• Encourage development and publicly reward high performance in staff members.
IV. Management Tasks
1. Priority setting
One of the most significant problems of management and leadership is the art of setting priorities for daily
work and projects because everything seems to be essential and requires attention and time. However, the
truth is that a lot of the work we do every day does not really need to be done, or at least not as important,
that needs to be done right away. Prioritisation of the work is critical. Simply defined, prioritisation is the
action or process of deciding the relative importance or urgency of a thing or things.
While prioritisation seems simple, i.e., knowing what needs to be done, it can be complex. It is essential to
take the following steps:
1.
Make a list of what needs to be done in the facility for the day, week, month or year.
SECTION EIGHT
Controlling Functions of the OIC
In the performance of its controlling functions, the OIC should;
1.
Continuously check progress against plans.
2.
Set up individual and organisational performance standards and see that they are achieved
per the plan.
3.
Detect irregularities either in data submitted or service delivery procedures, amongst
others.
4.
Identify opportunities for resource mobilisation and partnerships working in consultation
with the WDC and other stakeholders.
5.
Minimise costs and identify and eliminate wastages, particularly in the area of administrative
expenses and consumables and drugs.
6.
Develop coping strategies to deal with uncertainty regarding human, financial, and material
resources as they will never be in the necessary proportions.

2.
3.I
4.
5.
Highlight the ones that need to be completed urgently. These include things that, if not
done, will have negative consequences on the facility and quality of care provided
dentify which is most important, keeping in mind that the primary goal is to ensure an
efficient facility that offers patients quality care.
If activities have the same weight, start with the one that takes the most effort to complete
or is most beneficial to the patients.
Be aware that there could be uncertainties and changes, so know that priorities might
sometimes change, so be flexible but focused.
2. Setting and sharing objectives
People work well together when they agree with one another and share in setting and achieving the objectives
of the whole team. To fulfil the objectives of an organisation, those who work for the organisation should
know its objectives. People who have yet to share in setting objectives or been told what these objectives
are may waste much effort on activities that do not bring achieving objectives any nearer. People who oppose
the objectives may obstruct the organisation’s work. It is challenging, if not impossible, for real progress to
be made in health care without the active cooperation of everyone concerned. People do not give their
best when ordered to do things. One of the best ways, perhaps the only way to ensure that people agree on
objectives and that they are motivated to achieve them is to make sure that they take part in setting them.
3. Motivating team members
Motivation is an inner impulse that induces a person to act in a certain way. It is a series of internal drives
within a person at different levels:
Level 1: To obtain the necessities of life: food, shelter, clothing, rest and safety.
Level 2: To satisfy social needs such as companionship, love, and a position of respect.
Level 3: To ensure some degree of personal satisfaction and to pursue ideals. People need to feel
reasonably satisfied with themselves, what they make of their lives, and their talents and abilities.
A team leader should understand what encourages people to apply their ability and energy to work and what
makes people dissatisfied.These two groups of factors may be called motivators and dissatisfiers, respectively.
The six primary motivators in work are:
• Achievement: Most people like to do things well and succeed. Their satisfaction in success and in getting
things done well comes primarily from achieving what they expect to be able to achieve and what they aim
to achieve. Thus, achievement is significant to the individual. Good team leaders help their team members
achieve by giving them clear instructions, suitable training, and the necessary facilities and supplies.
•Recognition: Very few people are satisfied with simply knowing in their minds that they have been
SECTION EIGHT
successful. Most people like others to know about their success. Individuals can be sure that others know
of their success only when they respond to their achievements, i.e., acknowledge their success. It should be
easy to praise and publicise good work, but many leaders find it very hard to do so. Perhaps they fear their
achievements will be belittled if they recognise good work from those they lead. Lack of recognition can be
very discouraging; leaders should be willing to praise others when praise is due.
•The work itself: People like to do valuable and worthwhile work, be helpful to other people and help
themselves achieve their ideals.The staff of an organisation likes to do work that they can see as contributing
to the organisation’s objectives. The appeal of curative medical or caregiving work is self-evident. However,
health workers may find it hard to believe that such dull jobs as accurate record-keeping and inventory
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 355
control are worth doing. A good leader will try to keep dull, repetitive tasks to a minimum and take every
opportunity to assure the team members of the value of their work.
• Responsibility: To have responsibility is to accept the consequences of a decision or an action, good
or bad. Most people welcome responsibility; some fear it. Most people like to make decisions about their
lives and accept responsibility. A leader can quickly destroy or, at least, reduce staff members’ sense of
responsibility by making decisions for them. This should be avoided, especially when managing isolated clinics
where staff work primarily without direct supervision. A good leader takes the opportunity in such a clinic to
increase the staff’s sense of responsibility for work rather than diminish it with petty criticisms.
• Advancement: It is a form of recognition. Recognition without reward is not very convincing. People
prefer recognition that comes in a tangible form, such as an increase in salary or more responsibility, with
freedom to use their initiative, which leads to job satisfaction. People’s satisfaction often depends as much
on what they expect to get as on what they get. In acknowledging good work, the team leader or supervisor
does not make promises of advancement that are impossible to keep. Instead, he or she should encourage
people to increase their knowledge and skills to become ready for promotion.
• Self-improvement: People like to become mature and develop as people. Many make great sacrifices
to improve themselves and their families. Many people want opportunities to discover, develop, master,
and use their abilities to the fullest extent in their work and private lives. Since health workers deal with
people, there are endless opportunities for self-improvement. Team leaders can help their health workers
take such opportunities by providing learning material, challenging them with questions at work, helping them
set up research projects (which can be very simple), and giving them high-quality supervision. Given such
opportunities to develop, health workers will likely bring energy and enthusiasm to their work.
4. Delegating authority and responsibility
The leader of a team is responsible for the work the team undertakes.The leader must ensure that the team
has the necessary equipment and resources and that team members are adequately trained and can do the
work. The leader is responsible for the team’s failures and successes. At the same time, the health workers in
the team are responsible for the particular tasks of their jobs.
One way of using authority is to delegate it. To delegate means to give another person some of one’s
authority or, in other words, to give another person the power to make decisions.
Delegation has certain advantages:
•Delegating some decision-making saves time for other duties.
• When work is spread over a large area, as in rural health work, the health workers on the spot must be
able to make decisions according to circumstances.
• Delegation of responsibility saves long delays when awaiting decisions from a central office or other distant
• Health workers who can make decisions enjoy their work more and become more knowledgeable and
skilful.
It also has disadvantages:
• If wrong decisions are made, the work may not be done or done less well.
SECTION EIGHT
authority.
PAGE 356
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
•A leader who does not delegate properly may pass all the work on to the team members, leaving little more
to do.
•A leader may delegate decisions to people with insufficient experience.
