Rules for delegating authority and responsibility
1.
Be clear about precisely what is delegated.
2.
Select the person who can do the work.
3.
Explain to others the work that has been delegated and to whom.
4.
Only interfere if asked to, and be prepared for some mistakes.
5.
Give support as needed and follow up on the work progress.
5. Coordinating activities
Coordinating activities or groups of activities involves bringing them into proper relation with each other to
ensure that everything that needs to be done is done and that no two people are trying to do the same job.
To make coordination effective, seven well-recognised principles of organisation must be applied:
1.
Objective: The objective of each group of tasks must contribute to the organisation’s
objectives as a whole.
2.
Definition: Each group of tasks must be clearly defined so that everyone knows precisely
the tasks.
3.
Command: Each group of tasks must have one person in charge, and all concerned must
know who this person is.
4.
Responsibility: The person in charge of a team is responsible for the performance of its
members.
5.
Authority: Each person responsible for a group of tasks must have authority equal to the
responsibility.
6.
Span of control: No person in charge of a group of tasks should be expected to control
more than six to ten others.
7.
Balance: The person in charge of several groups must ensure that they are balanced. For
instance, case-finding must not be so extensive that more disease cases are found than can
be treated.
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 357
A coordinating checklist
A health worker responsible for an action (any action) will find it helpful to apply the following checklist:
What is to be done?
Where will the action take place?
When will the action take place?
What equipment is needed?
How will the action be arranged?
Which members of the health team will take part?
Who outside the health team will take part?
Who will do what?
Who will lead?
Coordinating the activities
Coordinating the people
Is all necessary information available? Has the information been communicated?
Communication
6. Communication
Successful teamwork hinges on strong relationships among team members, particularly between the team
leader and others. It is a common scenario that team dynamics can be intricate. Challenges often stem
from or are exacerbated by ineffective communication within the team and between the leader and team
members. Similarly, complex relationships can lead to or worsen poor communication. Therefore, a team
leader must prioritise the quality of team relations and communication to uphold positive relations.
To encourage communication, the team leader should always observe certain principles:
All team members should be free to express and explain their views and should be encouraged to do so.
Their unique perspectives and insights are invaluable to the team’s success, and their contributions should
be acknowledged and appreciated.
The team leader is also responsible for ensuring that all messages, whether spoken or written, are articulated
clearly and in a manner that all can comprehend.Your guidance and support can significantly boost the team’s
communication, making them feel supported and guided.
Communication is a two-way process - sending and receiving. When the message that is sent is not received,
communication has not taken place. Therefore, as a team leader, it is crucial always to use some means of
checking that the intended effect has taken place. This can be done through feedback, asking questions to
ensure understanding, or using visual aids to reinforce the message. By checking that the intended effect of
the communication has taken place, one can ensure effective communication and understanding, making the
team feel heard and considered.
How to manage space to assist communication
Always try to arrange rooms, offices, classrooms, other group-education spaces, tables, and seats to
communicate effectively. For instance, if the purpose of the meeting is to encourage open discussion, a circular
seating arrangement can facilitate equal participation. On the other hand, a theatre-style seating arrangement
can be more suitable if the meeting involves a presentation. The key is to adapt the seating arrangements to
the purpose of the meeting, not the other way around. This can help create a more conducive environment
SECTION EIGHT
Conflict or disagreement is a natural part of human relationships. However, it should be managed to yield
constructive outcomes. The team leader plays a pivotal role in facilitating open and respectful discussions,
promoting compromise, and ensuring that conflicts are resolved in a just and timely manner. This approach
fosters a sense of security and harmony within the team.
PAGE 358
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
for effective communication and engagement.
7. Conducting meetings
Meetings are a necessary part of health work, especially when the work is with rural communities. Meetings
are of many kinds and can have many different purposes. Large public meetings, often held in the open air,
may be held to encourage people to express their views on a new project or inform them of and explain
something new. Small meetings may be held with community leaders to identify health problems and needs.
Health education may be taught in meetings with special groups, such as patients or mothers.The health team
also has regular meetings. Sometimes, committee meetings are held to decide on a new project, educational
meetings, or discussion groups to learn new skills and approaches.
Preparing for a meeting
When preparing for a meeting, it is useful to check the following items:
• Purpose of meeting
•·Main subject-matter
•·Type of meeting
• Size of meeting
• Place, time, and duration of the meeting
•·Who is convening and organising the meeting
•·Announcements or information about the meeting.
Purpose: The purpose of the meeting should be clear. For a formal committee, the agenda should state
the purpose. However, it is worth writing a summary of the purpose, stating what it is hoped to achieve.
Understanding the purpose of the meeting is crucial as it helps you set the correct agenda, invite the relevant
participants, and prepare the necessary materials. Some meetings are called to communicate information,
others to exchange views and ideas, and others to make decisions about plans or activities.
Subject Matter: If a meeting is to be useful, each person present must have as much information as possible
about the subject to be discussed. Also, the facts, principles or ideas needed as a basis for the discussion
must be provided, sometimes before the meeting by means perhaps of a book or a working paper, or at the
meeting by a knowledgeable person or by use of a tape recording or a film. If the subject for discussion is
new to several group members, someone with exceptional knowledge of the subject should be asked to give
a brief introduction.
SECTION EIGHT
Type of Meeting: Meetings may be small or large, formal or informal, and open to all or only to members.
Size of the Meeting: The Size of a meeting largely determines how a subject is discussed, whether everybody
takes part, and whether it is easy to reach decisions or take votes. Two or three people may be enough when
the group is meeting to share ideas or for a spontaneous discussion, searching for new ideas. A group of
five to seven people is usually large enough to provide a reasonable variety of experiences and personalities
while enabling everyone to participate. Generally, a group should be at most ten to twelve people for group
discussion or training. Even with this number, a small subgroup may form and dominate the discussion, while
the rest of the group may become mere observers. Large meetings are more helpful in communicating
information and exchanging views than discussing or making decisions.
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 359
Place and Time: The choice of meeting place should avoid the need for most people to come a long way;
a hall or open square at the centre of a village is often the best place. The time chosen for a meeting is
significant. The time must suit the public rather than the organiser for a public meeting. People who work all
day prefer evening or weekend meetings.
The Convener and Organizer: The convener is the person who calls people together for a meeting and
maybe the health worker, a community leader, or the chairman of the group that is holding the meeting. The
convener plays a crucial role in setting the agenda, ensuring that all necessary participants are invited and
the meeting’s objectives are met. The organiser, who may be the same person as the convener or a helper,
makes the practical arrangements for the meeting, such as hiring or borrowing the meeting place, informing
the members, inviting the speakers, and providing information in advance about the meeting. Their roles are
essential in ensuring the meeting is well-organised and productive.
Announcements or Information about the Meeting: All those concerned with a meeting should know
about it in plenty of time. Notices about a meeting may be written invitations but are usually posted on walls
and doors in public places such as shops or post offices.
Conducting a committee meeting
How a committee meeting is conducted depends on whether it is large, small, formal, or informal. The
chairperson should be someone who can encourage good communication. In conducting any meeting, certain
factors must be kept in mind:
• The agenda should be followed.
• All participants should be allowed to speak.
• Decisions should be made based on consensus whenever possible.
These practices ensure that the meeting is productive and that all participants feel heard and valued.
The role of the chairman -The chairman keeps the meeting to its primary purpose, gives everyone a fair
chance to take part, controls the timing, and keeps order. There are three simple rules for group meetings:
1.
There must be no rudeness or personal remarks; no member must ever make another
appear foolish.
2.
The chairperson must have the absolute right to control the discussion, rule out irrelevant
remarks, and stop the proceedings if necessary.
3.
The chairperson should be able to keep the discussion going when necessary, e.g. by
raising questions or new topics. At a small meeting, everyone present should be stimulated
to take part.Those who are too talkative should be discouraged from talking too much, and
those who are hesitant about joining in should be encouraged.
SECTION EIGHT
Communication - The success of any meeting depends on the quality of communication. Suppose the
purpose is to pass on information or to explain something. In that case, speakers should find out whether it
has been successful, for example, by allowing questions and discussion, which will show whether the subject
has been understood. If the purpose is to seek the views of those present at the meeting, the chairperson
or secretary should summarise the views expressed or put them in different words to obtain agreement
about what has been said. Quarrels and shouting at meetings are not helpful and are often the result of poor
communication. When people understand each other clearly, there are fewer disagreements.
PAGE 360
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
Controlling the time -Controlling the time that discussions and questions take is essential to conducting
a meeting. Members’ questions and discussions must be kept within a definite time limit to give everyone
a chance to speak. If the subject permits or the discussion is likely to become lengthy, a definite time limit
should be set for a small meeting, with target times set at intervals. Using time in this way helps people bring
their ideas into focus. If a decision cannot be reached within the time limit set, it is advisable to postpone the
attempt, thus giving time for further thought or preparation.
Role of the Secretary -The secretary records the decisions. Like the chairperson, the secretary may be
appointed by those who set up the committee or elected by the committee members. The secretary’s main
task is to make a record of the main events of the meeting, and in particular to record:
•·The names of members present.
•·The names of any visitors.
•·The names of absent members and of those who apologised for their absence.
•·The exact wording of any decisions taken.
•·Summaries of discussions leading to these decisions.
•·The date of the next meeting.
In recording discussions, it is customary not to record the names of members who made particular points.
8. Dealing with problems and conflicts
Helping staff solve personal problems - A good supervisor tries to understand that health staff may
have financial problems (for instance, because a landlord has increased the rent) or worries because a child
is sick, a relative has died, or a child has failed an examination. Personal worries are many and varied and may
interfere with work. A good supervisor listens sympathetically to personal problems and, whenever possible,
tries to help solve them. A person whose problems are considered with sympathy can work better. One who
feels that a supervisor does not care and is unsympathetic may feel resentful and angry, which may affect the
quality of his or her work.
Dealing with disputes
Disputes often occur when people work together in groups and teams. The team leader or supervisor is
expected to prevent or settle them.
SECTION EIGHT
Preventing disputes
The most typical cause of disputes in a work team is confusion, caused by people having different ideas about
what should be done and how it should be done. Such disputes can be minimised or prevented by:
•·Frequent meetings of health team members
•·Allowing people to express views openly and letting the whole group decide what should be done.
•·Sharing agreed objectives.
•·Having clear and detailed job descriptions.
•·Having clear instructions and procedures to follow.
•·Distributing tasks fairly.
•·Creating work schedules that distribute work fairly.
• A common cause of argument is jealousy and favouritism. A supervisor must behave towards each team
member with complete fairness and justice and never publicly criticise staff.
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 361
Settling disputes
A serious argument can affect the morale and performance of the whole health team. Arguments must be
stopped quickly, and the people concerned must be reconciled.What should the supervisor do in such cases?
First, all the people involved in the argument should be separately interviewed so that all the relevant facts
are known. Second, every effort should be made to discover the actual cause of the argument.There is much
truth in the saying, “It takes two to quarrel”; while more blame may attach to one side than to the other,
there are likely to be faults on both sides. Third, the people involved should be asked - again separately - for
their views on how to resolve the argument and whether they are willing to be reconciled. If a solution is
acceptable to all concerned, productive and friendly working relations can be re-established. If agreement
cannot be reached, the best solution is to persuade those involved to ‘agree to differ’ - to acknowledge the
differences in their views but to stop arguing about them.
Summary;
•The supervisor tries to understand the personal problems of health workers
•The supervisor tries to prevent quarrelling and lousy feelings among health team members.
•The supervisor tries to reconcile people who quarrel, to bring them together.
•Clear instructions, job descriptions, and sympathetic supervision help prevent disputes.
V.Healthcare Facility Management Tool
1) Quarterly Business Plan
This is a quarterly document that describes all planned activities, needs, and requirements and estimates
costs across all key priority areas for the quarter in view. It is prepared by the facility and WDC with support
from the LGHA. When finalized, it is signed by the OIC and WDC chairman and submitted to the LGHA for
approval.
VI. Community Engagement in Governance of PHCs
Community Engagement (CE) is the process of working collaboratively with and through groups of people
affiliated by geographic proximity, particular interest, or similar situations to address issues affecting the
well-being of those people. It is a powerful vehicle for bringing about environmental and behavioural changes
that will improve the community’s and its members’ health. It often involves partnerships and coalitions that
help mobilize resources and influence systems, change relationships among partners, and serve as catalysts
for changing policies, programs, and practices. Therefore, community engagement in the governance of PHCs
seeks to work collaboratively with all the established groups in the catchment area, e.g. WDCs/VDCs. So,
community engagement is neither synonymous nor interchangeable with community mobilization, community
participation or community health programmes (CHPs).
Inclusion and Demographic Diversity: Equitably incorporate diverse people, voices, ideas and information to
lay the groundwork for quality outcomes and democratic legitimacy.
Collaboration and Shared Purpose: Support and encourage participants, government and community
institutions, and others to work together to advance the common good.
SECTION EIGHT
The Core Principles of Community Engagement are as follows;
Careful Planning and Preparation:Through adequate and inclusive planning, ensure that the design, organization,
and convening of the process serve both a clearly defined purpose and the needs of the participants.
PAGE 362
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
Openness and Learning: Help all involved listen to each other, explore new ideas unconstrained by
predetermined outcomes, learn and apply information to generate new options, and rigorously evaluate
community engagement activities for effectiveness.
Transparency and \Trust: Be transparent and open about the process, and provide a public record of the
organizers, sponsors, outcomes, and range of views and ideas expressed.
Impact and Action: Ensure each participatory effort has real potential to make a difference and that participants
are aware of that potential.
Sustained Engagement and Participatory Culture: Promote a culture of participation with programs and
institutions that support ongoing quality community engagement.
Activities that align with the community engagement approaches include:
1.
Provision of services, counselling and behavioural change communication through home
visits by Community Health Influencers, Promoters and Services (CHIPS) agents and other
mobilizers
2.
Promoting active male involvement through various interpersonal communication
strategies – community sensitization workshops and discussions.
3.
Town announcements by town announcers
4.
Mosque and church announcements.
5.
Compound meetings with heads of households, women, and other key stakeholders.
6.
Edutainment activities – Rallies and road shows, among others.
7.
Community dialogue is a process that encourages communities to plan and implement
their solutions to health issues.
Multiple channels should be used to complement community efforts (social and traditional media, production,
and distribution of IEG materials).
SECTION EIGHT
VII. Ward Health System:
The Ward Health System has embedded community engagement principles within it. It aims to improve
and ensure sustainable health services with full and active participation and involvement of people at the
grassroots level.The system has a governance mechanism through which communities are constantly engaged
and involved in governance; hence, it has the Ward Development Committee (WDC) at the ward level, the
Village Development Committee (VDC) at the village level, and the Facility Management Committee (FMC)
for every primary health care facility.
Ward Development Committee
The ward has the lowest level of healthcare and governance, and to be effective, the ward development
committee is supposed to be constituted to assist the PHCs in demand creation and health education and
provide an enabling environment for the PHC. The WDC serves as the apex body for the functions of the
PHC and other development activities carried out in the ward. It has a direct link with the LGHA through a
representative of the WDC serving on the LGHA Advisory Committee.
Roles and Responsibilities of the Ward Development Committee
1.
Identify the health and development needs of the ward and plan for them.
2.
Develop and forward the Community Action Plan (for the ward) to the LG Health
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 363
Authority.
3.
Identify local human and material resources to meet these needs.
4.
Ensure implementation of the Community Action Plan
5.
Ensure ownership and sustainability of programs and projects.
6.
Mobilize and stimulate active involvement of prominent and other local people in project
planning, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation.
7.
Take an active role in supervising and monitoring the Drug Revolving Fund (DRF) and the
proper use of free/donated health commodities in the ward.
8.
Funds should be raised for health and development programs at facility, village, and ward
levels when necessary.
9.
Ensure accountability by providing regular feedback to the ward on how funds raised are
utilized.
10.
Ensure that women, children and the poorest can access the health services they need.
11.
Liaise with local government and other voluntary agencies to find solutions to health and
development challenges in the ward.
12.
Supervise the activities of the VDCs/FMCs/CHEWs/JCHEWs/CHIPS Agents/CEFPs
13.
Oversee the functioning of all health facilities in the ward.
14.
Provide necessary support to CHIPS Agents and CEFPs.
15.
A dedicated bank account for the selected Primary Health Centre in the ward with a
reliable commercial bank for the BHCPF funds.The signatories shall be the WDC Chairman
and OIC Primary Health Centre
16.
Maintain a separate WDC bank account for WDC fundraising activities. The signatories
shall be the Chairman, Secretary, and Treasurer of the WDC
17.
Monitor essential medicines and consumables, essential equipment and infrastructure at
monthly intervals.
18.
Ensure the proper functioning of health facilities in the ward using a maintenance plan.
19.

Village Development Committee
The ward should be divided into a minimum of 2 and a maximum of 6 villages or neighbourhood areas (for
urban LGAs) based on either traditional authority or accessibility.

•Mobilize and stimulate active community involvement in the implementation of developed health plans.
•Determine exemptions for drug payment and deferment, but provide funds for the exemptions/deferments.
•VDC collaborates with health workers to determine the prices of essential drugs.
•Supervise all account books (Monies at hand should be deposited in a bank within 24 hours or 72 hours

Human Resources for Health Requirement for PHCs
The HRH requirements for PHC would vary depending on the type of PHC but the HRH requirements
according to the National Standards and Regulatory Framework for PHCs in Nigeria are shown in the Table
below:
Where the OIC has less than these minimum number of staff in the facility, continuous advocacy by the WDC
is recommended for government (LGHA/SPHCB) to recruit staff to make up for the number. However,
facilities that are benefiting from the BHCPF are allowed to hire additional staff after they must have hired
2 midwives. Generally, recruitment and hiring are carried out at state and LGA levels, not the health centre.
However, there are steps the officer-in-charge (OIC) can take to improve the chances of receiving the
number of staff needed at the health centre. These include:
1. Contact your LGHA office to learn the positions (number and cadre) assigned to your health
centre
• How does the number and cadre of positions actually assigned compare with recommended staffing (see
below)?
• Are any assigned positions unfilled at your health centre? If so, how many?
• Which positions are vacant, and for how long have they been vacant?
2. Locate job descriptions for each position assigned to your health centre
Job descriptions will help you determine the qualifications and positions for staff you wish to recruit to your
health centre.These job descriptions are usually standardized across health facilities, and should be on fi le at
your health centre or at your district health office. Once staff is hired, job descriptions can be used to help
assess employee performance.
3. Learn how the local hiring process works
Hiring is usually carried out by the State and the LGHA, but you can increase your odds of obtaining the
staff you need by: being informed about the hiring procedures that apply to your health centre, advocating
for your team, and pursuing alternative hiring procedures when needed particularly as it relates to engaging
the volunteers. For example, you may need to send an official request to the State PHCDA or LGHA create
a position or fill the vacancy.
4. Communicate regularly with the local recruiting authorities
Keep in contact with people in the personnel office at your LGHA so that you know of upcoming changes
that could affect staffing of positions at your health centre. Keep these people informed of your staffing
needs, using the information you gathered, and citing the vacancies that exist and the recommended staffing
tables above to justify your need for additional staff.
SECTION EIGHT
5. Be persistent!
If budget ceilings or other limitations prevent you from hiring staff:
•Ask the WDC to hire qualified temporary staff from their internally generated income/contributions.
•Try alternative procedures. Contact local NGOs and donors to ask if they can hire and pay the salaries for
new staff at your health centre.
•Recruit volunteers. Make sure you develop good relations with local communities and community groups
so you can recruit volunteers in times of high workload (see below).

Developing Duty Rosters
A duty roster is a roster that schedules staff based on tasks and duties. This type of roster ensures that you
have particular duties covered. It is unlike regular rosters that scheduling is based on availability and hours.
Please see annex I for sample Duty Roster template. Duty rosters are important because they help schedule
staff based on your business’s demand instead of your team’s availability.
SECTION EIGHT
Principles of developing duty rosters
The principles are as follows:
a)
Delivery of service to patients is always the first consideration when developing a roster.
b)
Rosters must ensure that there is sufficient and appropriately skilled staff rostered to work
in order to provide appropriate patient care and to meet anticipated service demands.
c)
Rosters must conform to relevant regulatory frameworks, including antidiscrimination,
work health and safety legislation among others
d)
Rosters should ensure staff is treated fairly, while still providing appropriate flexibility to
facilitate meeting unit staffing needs.
e)
Rosters must make appropriate provision for adequate staff supervision, training and clinical
handover
f)
Developing rosters is based on co-operation between the managers (OIC in this case) and
staff, in order to promote fairness and to deliver appropriate care to patients.
Steps in developing Duty Rosters
Step I: Start by listing all the tasks and services provided by the health facility. This will vary depending on
whether it is type 1, II or III PHC.
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 373
Step II: Track when these tasks occur. Think about the tasks listed you created and when the tasks/services
are most likely to be performed/demanded. It could be during morning, afternoon and evening of a day or
what days of the week or month.
Step III: Schedule based on skill. After determining the tasks and the required period of coverage (hours/
days), you must schedule staff for each duty based on their skill and qualifications.
A suggested template for developing duty roster is found in Annex .
Task Shifting and Task Sharing
Task shifting is “the rational redistribution of tasks among health workforce teams. Specific tasks are
moved, where appropriate, from highly qualified health workers to health workers with shorter training and
fewer qualifications in order to make more efficient use of the available human resources for health.”
Task sharing is allowing a wider range of cadres to offer certain services, when this can be done safely
and effectively as a means of rapidly expanding access and improving health care. The goal of task shifting or
sharing is simply “to get the right workers with the right skills in the right places doing the right things”4
without necessarily abandoning other methods of increasing the number of qualified health workers It is the
policy of training specific health cadre on tasks that are ordinarily meant for other health cadres (Particularly
those above them) for them to perform such tasks when those that are designated for such tasks are
not available. For instance, Voluntary Health Workers can be trained on counseling to enable them counsel
pregnant women in the absence of a substantive counselor.
Please find in Annex II a table indicating tasks that may be performed by different cadre of staff.
Below are steps on how to make “task-shifting” effective:
Establish a clinical “team-based approach” through regular clinical team meetings and good
communications between staff. Conduct a weekly meeting of all staff at which you can
openly discuss patient cases and issues that arise, and work together to solve problems.
Encourage regular dialogue between staff about how to improve tasks to increase service
efficiency and quality.
Establish regular performance measurements to assure adherence to clinical and other
standards (see Quality Improvement chapter) Implement strategies to motivate your staff
and to prevent ‘burnout’. When staff is required to take on new tasks in an already heavy
workload, they can suffer increased anxiety, stress and burnout. Work together as a team
to determine how you can keep each other motivated. Task shifting can be a real asset to
your health centre, but it takes teamwork, supervision and constant communication!
Using Job Descriptions
The distribution of tasks among the members of a health team is one of a manager’s most important functions.
When work is distributed unfairly it causes dissatisfaction and sometimes quarrelling. Health tasks are very
varied. A nurse, for example, may have to take temperatures, deliver babies, conduct clinics and write reports.
This variation in tasks makes organization in health work a challenge to the team leader. Job descriptions are
one means of helping to distribute tasks among the health team. The purpose of job descriptions is to define
exactly for the holders of different posts, their fellow workers and their supervisors:
• What the holders of the posts are expected to do.
SECTION EIGHT
1.
2.
PAGE 374
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
• What standards they are expected to reach.
• To whom they are responsible.
• Whose work they supervise.
Uses of Detailed Job Descriptions
Job descriptions are a valuable tool for the organization of work. A job description:
a)
States clearly what each health worker must do and is expected to achieve.
b)
Help prevent arguments between people about who should do what. They help also in the
distribution of the equipment needed to do the work.
c)
Help prevent gaps and overlaps. It often happens that certain tasks are not done because
nobody accepts responsibility for them. These gaps can be prevented by a clear job
description for each team member. Without specific job descriptions, two people may
think each is responsible for the same job. Such confusion should be avoided.
d)
Can show the need for training, for instance if it includes a duty for which the health
worker has not been trained nor needs further training.
e)
Are useful as a basis for evaluating team members’ performance. However, they should be
interpreted flexibly, as guidelines, rather than too strictly or literally. Thus, team members
should be ready to help with one another’s work when necessary.
Contents of a Job Description
A job description should be written under specific headings to make sure that it gives all the necessary
information.
Job title: This is the standard title for the person doing the work or job, e.g. Nurse/midwife, Grade 2.
Date: The date is included because a job description is not final. People and roles change, and job
descriptions should be reviewed and, if necessary, revised at least once a year.
Job summary: This is a brief summary of the main responsibilities of the job.
SECTION EIGHT
Duties: This is the central and most important part of the job description. Each duty should be an identifiable
entity, are cognizable part of the jobholder’s work. Each duty should correspond to one or more programme
objectives, which should be listed. The health worker can then see how his or her duties contribute to the
improvement of the health of the community.
Relations: These are simple statements concerning:
The title of the person to whom the jobholder is accountable. For a particular task one
person can be responsible to only one superior. However, one person can hold more than
one job, and for different jobs may be responsible to different superiors.
The titles of people supervised by the jobholder. For example, a nurse/midwife may
supervise several community nurses in different villages. She is responsible for seeing
that they perform their work properly.
Qualifications: A section on qualifications describes the basic training and level of experience required for
the job.
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 375
Training and Development:
Every job description should be accompanied by a programme for
the further training and development of the person holding the job. This may be, for example, a regular
programme of reading or of in-service training, and opportunities to attend professional meetings. Like every
other element of the job description, this should be discussed in full and worked out with the jobholder.
Review and appraisal: This is a statement describing the process for review and appraisal of the performance
of the jobholder. Normally the supervisor is expected to carry out such a review periodically. The review
and appraisal statement should state clearly who has this responsibility. The appraisal may take the form of
an annual confidential report written by the supervisor, a simple statement that work is satisfactory, or a
recommendation for a change in the duties, or the promotion, of the job holder.
How to Outline Duties
The duties of a job are usually determined by:
Tradition and training. These define how similar jobs have been done previously or are done elsewhere. For
example, midwives are expected to perform certain duties according to the way they are trained and the
way other midwives practise.
The interpretation of the person who holds the job. Experience often leads people to change their way of
work or to adapt to new circumstances. For example, a midwife in a village must work in a different way from
a midwife in a large hospital.
Because rural health work and community work are developing and changing, supervisors and health workers
should keep job descriptions under review, so that they remain useful. One way to do this is to get health
workers themselves to define their duties.
A supervisor will find it very useful to ask health workers to list their duties and then to discuss these duties
with them; it may be possible to rearrange or reallocate certain duties so that they are performed more
efficiently.
The requirements of programmes and services.The objectives and strategies of the services and programmes
in which the health workers are employed are the determining factors in the definition of duties and tasks.
Steps needed to manage training at health centre:
1.
Identify training needs:
Review your training logs to identify the required and optional training
courses that each staff member currently needs.
2.
Identify available training: Contact the LGHA office, State hospitals and donors in your area to
identify available training that matches your needs. Does the training take place on-the-job or
offsite? Is any follow-up support provided? Is training accredited? Determine how training will be
paid for, including fees, travel, and meals.
SECTION EIGHT
Ensuring staff have appropriate training
As the in-charge you play an important role in planning and tracking the training your staff receive.You should
ensure that health centre staff has the right training at the right time to provide the quality basic services
outlined in this manual. You also should ensure that training opportunities are provided fairly and do not
interfere with service delivery. By helping your staff gain access to training opportunities in an equitable way,
you also help promote their career development and improve motivation and morale. The following steps
are recommended:
PAGE 376
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
3.
Determine how you will maintain service delivery while your staff is away at training.
4.
When training is completed, keep a record of it in a training log. This log can be completed
by a clerk, but as supervisor you should ensure that this recordkeeping happens on a
regular basis.
5.
Training debriefing: Ask staff who received the training to share key lessons learned and
training ‘likes and dislikes’ with other health centre team members. This can take place
during a team lunch or meeting.
6.
Do not be afraid to ask. If the LGA or donors do not provide the training your staff needs,
tell them what you need. They may be able to help - lend you staff from the LGA to fill in
for your staff while they are on training.
Improving staff motivation and retention
Employee satisfaction is directly linked to employee motivation, performance and quality of care. If you
do not pay attention to employee satisfaction, your staff will be disgruntled and perform poorly. Unhappy
employees can lead to unhappy and dissatisfied patients. The OIC needs to consider the following:
1.
Provide opportunities for staff to grow and develop (participating in training, mentorship,
new tasks, and chances to be promoted)
2.
Treat staff fairly (fairness in how praise or criticism is delivered, and how training, salaries,
promotions, and other opportunities are provided)
3.
Ensure staff has clear job descriptions and appropriate tools to complete the tasks.
4.
Provide regular feedback to staff on his/her performance.
5.
Share with staff how their work contributes to the team and to the health of patients.
Staff Handing Over Notes
Staff movement (redeployment, retirement, transfers etc) are realities of the workplace but which may result
in disruption of workflow if not properly handled. The officer-in-charge should ensure that proper handing
over notes is prepared by the departing or out-going staff for the in-coming. The comprehensiveness of such
notes and the diligence in which it is prepared will greatly assist the new staff in settling down to his/her
duties.
SECTION EIGHT
Follow the following steps:
Step 1. Discuss with the handover with the employee
Step 2. Draw up an early outline of your handover document (use a standard template see annex
3)
Step 3. Staff tie up loose ends (clear all outstanding issues)
Step 4. Communicate with your colleagues (for any areas to highlight)
Step 5. Complete a formal handover document with sample content below:
Step 6. Allow as much time as possible for a full handover.
Step 7. Hand over key documents personally
Step 8. Make the effort to identify and help resolve any occurring issues.
Step 10. Have a final handover meeting.

8.2 FINANCIAL MANAGEMENT IN PHC
8.2.1 Overview (Reasons, Principles, Pillars, Tools)
For PHCs financial management entails planning, organizing, controlling, and monitoring the financial resources
of the health Centre to improve service delivery and accountability. The OIC is ultimately responsible for
managing the finances of the PHC, however, every staff member and the WDC are also accountable and
should help in ensuring the effective controls are in place.
At the heart of financial management is the concept of financial control. This describes a situation where the
financial resources of the health facility are being correctly and effectively used. Financial management will
only happen if strong and relevant financial policies and procedures are put in place. Poor financial control in
the health facility means that:
•Assets will be put at risk of theft, fraud, or abuse.
•Funds may not be spent in accordance with the NGO’s objectives or donors’ wishes and
•The competence of managers may even be called into question.
Ideally, an Accounts Officer or Clerk is responsible for the accounting function of healthcare facilities, which
includes recording of all financial transactions related to assets, liabilities, income, and expenses of the PHC
and maintaining the books of accounts in a complete and accurate manner. At a minimum, education up to
ordinary diploma (ND) level is required to take on this position. An accounting clerk job usually requires
a few years of experience in accounting duties, and a firm understanding of numbers and ledgers and how
they relate to invoices and receipts. However, if the PHC does not have an Accounts Officer or Clerk, they
may want to request help from the LGHA accounts clerk, and it that is not possible, the OIC must take
responsibility.
Decentralised Facility Financing (DFF)
As part of efforts to address the limited funding of PHCs, the Basic Health Provision Fund (BHCPF) was
established in the 2014 National Health Act. A percentage of the resource under BHCPF was earmarked to
improve PHC service delivery. NPHCDA adopted the Decentralised Facility Financing (DFF) an innovative
and tested approach for improving quality of primary health delivery in Nigeria. DFF entails direct transfer of
funds to facilities and giving PHC facilities some level of autonomy in the management and use of resources
for improved service delivery.
Three key principles guide the implementation of DFF:
Facility autonomy:The principle of facility autonomy ensures a health facility has the status to receive, manage
and account for funds from any legal source or funds flow. Increasing facility autonomy requires strengthening
SECTION EIGHT
The financial management process is continuous process of: Plan, Do and Review.
PAGE 378
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
governance including realigning institutional roles and relationships across all levels of government. Facility
autonomy can help to establish or invigorate the governance structure of facilities through the separation of
financial management responsibilities such as authorization and payments. Increased community participation
in planning can create new civic space to enhance transparency, accountability and engagement.
Purchase health services using output-based payment. The output-based payment principle enables health
purchasers to “buy the right thing” and better match payment to prioritized services. Output-based payment
permits health facilities to determine and procure the best mix of inputs to produce and deliver service
outputs to clients and communities.
Sound facility financial management.The autonomy to directly receive funds paid for outputs and the flexibility
to manage procurement of inputs to produce and deliver desired services must be underpinned by sound
health facility financial management and accountability. Establishment of good financial management systems
and processes at provider level enables facilities to diligently account for the inputs procured, report on the
use of funds and provide an audit trail to facilitate financial accountability. Health facilities can function as
businesses or management entities when they take advantage of these robust financial management systems
and processes.
The autonomy principle delegates to health facilities the right to procure inputs to produce and deliver
outputs, the output-based payment (financing) principle determines what facilities can procure, and the good
financial management principle sets the guidelines for how facilities procure with associated internal controls.
The Immunization Plus & Malaria Progress by Accelerating Coverage and Transforming Services (IMPACT)
project was conceptualized, with funding from a World Bank IDA credit facility, to support the Government
of Nigeria’s commitment to improving its human capital with the goal of reducing under-five mortality by 40
percent over the next decade. More importantly, both interventions have within their mandate a decentralized
facility financing system for PHC operations through direct funds transfer to each health facility.
Performance and Financial Management Officers (PFMOs)
Therefore, to help optimize PHC performance and improve financial accountability and transparency, the
Performance and Financial Management Officers (PFMOs) are engaged to serve as performance improvement
TAs and fiduciary agents responsible for overseeing, co-managing, monitoring and evaluating PHC performance
against all financial inflows to primary health care centers nationwide, prioritizing IMPACT and BHCPF
facilities which are currently receiving DFF.The Performance and Financial Management Officers engaged and
domiciled within the PHC Department, LGA Headquarters, will provide TA to PHCs and LGAs on the use,
documentation and reporting of PHC funds. It is expected that they will support PHC facilities and LGA in
BHCPF and IMPACT States; working with State PIUs and States Projects Financial Monitoring Unit (SPFMU)
in conducting sound financial management processes in all the PHC facilities that are beneficiaries of BHCPF,
IMPACT funds and other resources, in every LGA.
SECTION EIGHT
The PFMOs will promote a collaborative, client-oriented approach consistent with the public financial rules
and regulations and shall focus on the following:
•Evidence-based performance management of PHC programmes
•Support the LGA Finance Officers for the management of financial documentation of PHC facilities (i.e.,
retirement documentations)
•Financial knowledge management and capacity building of PHC financial focal persons and LGAs finance
officers
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 379
•Providing financial advice and guidance to PHC officers-in-charge within their LGA of assignment
•Financial management, financial reporting and controls
The PFMO directly impacts the effective management and financial integrity of primary health care centers
through the effective stewarding of government and partner resources including the World Bank IMPACT
funds, maintaining appropriate controls and integrity of financial management in accordance with relevant
rules and regulations, enabling prudent management financial resources and building an informed and shared
management engagement on financial issues in support of the PHC revitalization strategy. Also, the PFMOs
will be responsible for supporting programmatic performance improvement across the selected facilities.
Specific programmatic and performance indicators will be included in the TORs and KPIs of the PFMOs.
FMOs Scope of Work
Under the guidance and supervision of the SPHCB (IMPACT PIU and BHCPF Core team) and NPHCDA, the
PFMO’s duties include: Technical support to PHC facility finance officers and ensure:
•Oversight of the PHC facility finance officers, establishing strategic team objectives, aligning with BHCPF
key performance indicators, measurement standards, and expected results, offering guidance to enable
responsible, effective and efficient performance.
•Effective planning and allocation of resources in line with the facility business plan and ensure the cost
effective and appropriate use of resources in accordance with financial standards of ethics, integrity and
accountability framework.
•Continuous assessment of the performance and effectiveness of financial services and recommending
changes and/or corrective actions in the finance operations to enhance and maintain effectiveness in changing
environment.
•Fostering a positive work environment, respectful of both men and women, and ensure that the highest
standards of conduct are observed.
Financial Knowledge Management and Capacity Building
•Build the capacity of the primary health care facilities’ Finance Officers/Officers-in-Charge on financial
management in line with the health facility Financial Management guidelines.
•Lead, organize and conduct of trainings or workshops to build capacity of stakeholders on financial
accounting, including the identification of expert resources for the trainings/ workshops, synthesis and
dissemination of lessons learned and best practices in Finance, and contribute to knowledge networks and
communities of practice.
•Coach and mentor, the health facilities’ Finance Officers and Officers in Charge on proper financial
management and supervise related financial activities.
including support with development of quarterly business plans and use of financial management tools
deployed at the facility.
•Provide hands-on support to the health facility management and finance officers to function as required.

• Track all funds (investment funds, DFF, capitation etc.) disbursed to the health facilities and all administrative

Terms of Engagement
This engagement at this stage is for a period of one (1) year.
Reasons for Good Financial Management
• To be accountable, funders with good financial reporting systems, it is easier to show fund providers that
funds are being used for the purpose intended.
• To be accountable to the communities we work with We have a moral obligation to show that funds raised
in the community’s name are being used correctly.
• To be able to produce financial statements for funders, regulatory, supervisory bodies and other stakeholders
As part of the transparency and accountability process, PHCs may be required to be accountable for the
money they raise and spend.
• To minimise fraud, theft and abuse of resources Good financial management includes internal controls.
When these are in place, they help to stop fraud and protect the staff as well as the assets.
SECTION EIGHT
• To plan for the future and become more financially secure We have to plan to make sure we have enough
money to carry out our objectives now and in the future. Budgets help us plan for activities and manage cash.
Financial information helps us to identify potential financial risks and the need for savings (reserves).We need
financial information about where we are now and where we want to be in the future, to help identify our
long-term financing needs.
• To enable health facilities to make better decisions on the use of funds Complete, up-to-date and timely
reports enable managers to plan their activities according to the budget available and take decisions to fulfil
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 381
objectives. Good cash flow management enables activities to be planned, items purchased when needed.
• To achieve the objectives of the organisation Good financial management will give the management team
the information they need to ensure they are fulfilling their objectives and following the budget.
• To enjoy confidence and trust from their stakeholders Health facilities that keep good accounts, create
great budgets and produce accurate and timely financial reports, receive confidence and trust from their
stakeholders.
• To strengthen fundraising efforts Health facilities that present good budgets and financial reports with
funding proposals are more likely to receive a favourable response.
• To get value for our money Financial information allows us to compare and assess spending plans to make
sure we make efficient and effective use of financial resources.
Principles of Financial Management
Use these guiding principles as standards to assess your current practice and goals to aim for.
Consistency: Consistent use of financial policies and procedures are important for efficient operations. This
assists the financial reporting process and promotes transparency (one of the best ways to hide irregularities
is to change the way figures are reported).
Accountability: This is the moral or legal duty, placed on health facilities to explain how funds and equipment
in their custody have been utilized for the benefit of all stakeholders.
Allowability: Basic financial management cost principles define any expenditure incurred as either allowable
or unallowable. Allowable costs are expenditures incurred that can be covered under the terms of the funds
received grant. Unallowable costs are expenditures incurred that cannot be covered or reimbursed under
the funding agreement.
Transparency: PHCs must be open about their work, providing information about activities and plans to all
stakeholders.This includes preparing accurate, complete and timely financial reports. If an organization is not
transparent, it may give the impression they have ‘something to hide’.
Viability: To be financially viable, a PHC’s spending must be kept in balance with money coming in, both at the
operational and the strategic levels. Viability is a measure of the PHC’s financial continuity and security, the
Business Plan will help in ensuring financial viability of PHCs.
Stewardship: Financial stewardship involves taking good care of the financial resources we are entrusted
with, to make sure they are used for the intended purposes.The WDC has overall responsibility for this, they
should work together with staff in assessing financial risks and setting up appropriate systems and controls.
Accounting Standards: The system for keeping financial records and documentation must meet generally
accepted accounting procedures. Any person from anywhere around the world should be able to understand
your financial accounting systems.
SECTION EIGHT
Integrity: On a personal level, individuals must operate with honesty and propriety. Staff and WDC members
must lead by example in following policy and declare personal interests that might conflict with their official
duties. The integrity of financial records and reports is dependent on the accuracy and completeness of
financial records.
PAGE 382
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
Pillars of financial management
The following are four basic building blocks which must be in place to achieve good practice in financial
management:
Accounting records: Every organization must keep an accurate record of financial transactions that take place
to show how funds have been used. Accounting records also provide valuable information about how the
organization is being managed and whether it is achieving its objectives.
Financial planning: Link to the PHCs business and operational plans, the budget is the cornerstone of any
financial management system and plays an important role in monitoring the use of funds.
Financial monitoring: Providing the organization has set a budget and has kept and reconciled its accounting
records in a clear and timely manner, it is then possible to produce financial reports for all stakeholders.
Internal budget monitoring reports help managers to monitor the progress of projects and annual financial
statements provide accountability to external stakeholders.
Internal control: A system of controls, checks and balances – collectively referred to as internal controls
– are put in place to safeguard an organization’s assets and manage internal risk. Their purpose is to deter
opportunistic theft or fraud and to detect errors and omissions in the accounting records. An effective
internal control system also protects staff involved in financial tasks.
Tools of Financial Management
There are many tools, not necessarily financial, which managers can use to help achieve good practice in
financial management and control. We can identify these tools under each of the four functions of financial
management (as included in the definition above):
Planning is basic to the management process and involves looking ahead to prepare as well as possible for the
future. In the course of putting a plan together managers will consider several possible alternatives and make
a number of choices or decisions. Planning must always precede the doing.Tools for planning include business
plan, activity plan, budgets, work plans, cash flow forecast etc.
Organizing The resources of the organization – staff and volunteers, vehicles, property, money – have
to be coordinated to ensure implementation of the overall plan. It needs to be clear what activities and
responsibilities are to be undertaken, when and by whom. The tools used include organisation charts, flow
diagrams, job descriptions, Chart of Accounts, Finance Manual, budget etc.
SECTION EIGHT
Controlling A system of controls, checks and balances are essential to ensure proper application of
procedures and resources during programme implementation. Tools used include Budgets, delegated
authority, procurement procedure, reconciliation, internal and external audit, fixed assets register, vehicle
policy, insurance etc.
Monitoring This involves producing regular and timely information for managers and stakeholders for
monitoring purposes. Monitoring involves comparing actual performance with plans to evaluate the
effectiveness of plans, identify weaknesses early on and take corrective action if required. Tools used include
evaluation reports, budget monitoring reports, cash flow reports, financial statements, project reports, donor
reports, audit reports, evaluation report etc.
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 383
8.2.2 Financial Record Keeping at PHC
The Chart of Accounts
There are many kinds of financial transaction taking place in a health facility, to make sense of all of this
financial activity, it helps to ‘sort’ the different types of transaction into a series of predetermined descriptive
categories or ‘accounts. These accounts are listed in the Chart of Accounts and are typically arranged in a
logical order as follows:
• Income (money received by the facility such statutory allocation, see below)
• Expenditure (things we spend money on such as repairs, fuel, utilities commodities
• Assets (things we own such as vehicle, equipment, stock of drugs etc.).
• Liabilities (things we owe such as utility bills payable, vendor for supplies not yet paid etc.)
Income of PHCs
It is advised that each funding source should have a separate bank account and cashbook, the income sources
include the following:
• Gateway (monthly) operational funds: Received from NPHCDA through the SPHCDAs to PHCs. This
should have a dedicated bank account.
• NHIA reimbursement
• Revenue from charges for services not covered under the BMPHS
• Sale of drugs under the Drug Revolving Fund (DRF)
• Statutory allocation from state budgets received through the SPHCB and LGHA
• Others (including funds received from community members, development partners and non-state actors).
The conditions for receipt of the Gateway funds and the NHIA reimbursement are that the facility must
have been selected by the State under the principle of one PHC per political ward and is accredited under
the BHCPF or empaneled by the State Health Insurance Agency that services can be accessed at the facility.
Expenditures
• Provision and maintenance of facilities, equipment & transportation.
• Cost of fuel and diesel for power generators and vehicles
• Cost of human resources (training and hiring of ad-hoc staff and allowances)
• Procurement of essential drugs and commodities.
For small PHCs with very few financial transactions, a simple bookkeeping system is all that is needed,
refer to figure 8 above. As an organization grows and takes on a number of projects and different sources
of funding, its reporting requirements, and therefore its financial systems, will become more sophisticated.
Accounting records fall into two main categories: – Supporting Documents Every organisation should keep
files of the following original documents to support every transaction taking place:
• Receipt or voucher for money received.
• Payment voucher for money paid out.
SECTION EIGHT
Recording Income and Expenditure in the Cashbook
Which accounting records to keep
PAGE 384
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
• Invoices – certified and stamped as paid.
• Bank paying-in vouchers stamped and dated when money is taken to the bank.
• Bank statements.
• Journal vouchers – for one-off adjustments and non-cash transactions.
With these documents on file, it will always be possible to construct a set of accounts. Other useful supporting
documents include:
• Local Purchase Orders (LPOs)
• Goods Received Notes (GRNs).
Books of Account
Each facility should keep record of all financial transactions. The bookkeeping tools described below should
be available and used in PHCs:
• Bank (or Cash) Book for each bank account: To record all receipts and payments of all cash, cheques
and bank transfers of all financial transactions of a health facility. It is important to ensure that all financial
transactions pass through the bank accounts for accountability completeness of the accounting records.
• Cheque Register: This is used to record all cheques issued and received by the facility.
• Fixed Assets Register: This is where all assets bought or donated to the facility are recorded. The register
spells out the type of asset, cost (or market value where cost is not known especially if donated), quantity,
condition, and location of the asset. It is important that assets bear special identifying marks (tags) to
distinguish them.
• Bank Reconciliation Statements:These show reconciled figures between the cash book and bank statements.
It should be prepared monthly (at the latest) for every bank account operated by the facility.
• Purchase Day Book: This is used to record credit transactions. It contrasts with the cashbook which
records cash/cheque/transfers. It is written from invoices received from suppliers and vendors.
Recording in the Cashbook
All money that comes into the facility in the form of cash, cheques or bank transfers, and expenses incurred
should be recorded in the Cash Book.The PHC can purchase a pre-printed cashbook from a stationary store,
or a hard cover notebook and draw out the cashbook as shown below. Take the following steps:
• On receiving notification from bank of receipt of funds (e.g., NPHCDA Gateway, NHIS Pathway
reimbursement etc.), obtain the statement.
• Record the receipt (using same description as on bank statement) on the left-hand side (Debit) of the cash
SECTION EIGHT
book.
• Record payments on the right-hand side (credit) referencing the PV number used for the transactions. It is
important that details in the cashbook should correspond with that of the payment voucher.
• Sum up the entries in left-hand side (incomes) and insert the amount in row where you have Total. Balance
carried down (c/d) is the difference between the total income and the total payments.
• Sum up all the entries on the right-hand side included the balance c/d and insert in the cell next to Total.
The Balance b/d is the same as the Balance c/d and is the figure that begins the next month’s entries.
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 385
8.2.3 Estimating Costs for Business Plan, Monthly Expenditure Reporting and Quarterly
Financial Reports
Financial Reporting
PHCs are expected to develop plans and report on the implementation of such plans.The Quarterly Business
Plan, Monthly Expenditure Report and the Quarterly Financial Reporting are essential reports expected at
the PHC level.
Preparing Monthly Expenditure Reports
The report is prepared by health facility account clerk, it is signed by the OIC and the WDC Chairman and
submitted to the LGHA for approval and implementation support.
•Complete General Information Table: this captures the basic data such as name of health facility,Ward, LGA,
State and Zone. It also captures the month of the year under review and the date the plan was developed.
•Complete Plan of Activities Table: this captures the relevant information regarding any activity planned for
the month. The required fields are explained thus:
•Needs/requirements: these include all items and services required for the planned activity to be carried out
as part of strategy implementation process. Please refer to the Expenditure Categories below for guidance.
•Description of Activity: this involves a brief narrative of the planned activity. Please refer to the Expenditure
Categories below for guidance and consistency.
•Cost: this indicates the amount required for executing the activity. Please refer to the section on Budgeting
below for guidance on how to estimate costs.
•Beneficiary: indicates the vendor/individual to which the amount indicated will be paid to render the service/
make procurements to execute the activity. e. Beneficiary’s Contact Details: this includes the house/business
address and phone number(s) of the vendor/individual to whom money is paid to render the service/make
procurements.
Preparing Quarterly Financial Reporting
Please note that for ease of reporting, timeliness, completeness and accuracy, the total figures to be reported
should be obtained from the individual expense ledgers using the sum of the monthly totals for the months
in the quarter. For instance, the amount for stationeries for Quarter ending March 31 will be made up of
total stationeries expense figures for January, February and March). The following steps should be followed:
•Complete General Information Table: this captures the basic data such as name of health facility,Ward, LGA,
State and Zone. It also captures the months and the year of last quarter and current quarter under review
and the date the plan was developed.

1.

2.

4.
Revenue from other services (revenues from charges on patients for services not
covered under the BMPHS etc.
•Complete Expenses Table: this captures the projected and actual expenses for the last quarter and the
projected expenses for the quarter under review for the facility such as:

•Complete Revenue Table: this captures the projected and actual revenues for the last quarter and the

•Human resource development (total cost incurred on training or hiring of staff)

Procurement of essential drugs and commodities (total incurred on purchase of drugs and
