• Complete Summary Table: this shows a summary of the total revenues and expenses of the health facility.
It captures total projected and actual revenues and expenses for the last quarter and total projected for the
current quarter under review. It also shows the cash and bank balance at the end of the last quarter brought
forward into the current quarter.
Preparing Budgets/Cost Estimates
A budget describes an amount of money that an organisation plans to raise and spend for a set purpose
over a given period of time. It is a financial plan that relates directly to the activities of the organisation, it is
important to involve those who will be responsible for implementing in writing the budget. If this is not done,
the budget could be less accurate and staff less likely to appreciate the need to spend within budget or to
reach fund-raising targets.
Things to be aware of when estimating costs
A good practice in the budgeting process should imbibe the following:
• Clarity: Since many different people will need to use the budget for different purposes, they should be able
to understand it (and adapt it, when necessary) without any additional explanation beyond what is written
down. So, keep notes on budgeting assumptions and calculations.
• Get organised: There are several stages involved in constructing a budget before it can be submitted for
approval to the WDC, so it is a good idea to prepare a budgeting timetable and commence the process early.
This could be up to three months before the start of the financial year, depending on the size of the PHC and
what approach has been adopted.
• Budget Headings: One way of achieving consistency is to design a Budget Preparation Sheet for your facility,
which will act as a memory-jogger and prompt staff to include all relevant costs. It will list all of the main types
of income and expenditure that a PHC might have in a typical year.
• Estimating Costs: It is important to be able to justify calculations when estimating costs. Do not be tempted
SECTION EIGHT
to simply take last year’s budget and add a percentage amount on top for inflation. While last year’s budget
could be very helpful as a starting point, it could also be very misleading and contain historical inaccuracies.
From this detailed working sheet, it is then a simple matter to produce a summarised budget for each line
item. It is also easy to then update if quantities or unit costs change. See below for detailed guidance on how
to use a budget worksheet.
• Contingencies: Contingencies are items included in a budget for unforeseen expenses. It is best to include
these for the budget lines that might need a ‘cushion’ (e.g. fuel) rather than adding a ‘bottom line’ percentage.
Every item in your budget must be justifiable – adding a percentage on the bottom is difficult to justify – and
difficult to monitor.
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 387
• Forgotten costs: There is a tendency to under-estimate the true costs of running a health facility due to
omission of some costs such as: i. Vehicle running costs. ii. Equipment maintenance and repairs (e.g. for airconditioners, photocopiers and computers) iii. Governance costs (e.g. WDC meetings) iv. Book-keeping and
Audit fees etc.
• Check the figures: Double check and ask colleagues to check your draft cost estimates with a critical eye.
Ask them to check the figures for accuracy, that you have included all of the resources needed for the facility
and if the total cost looks reasonable.
Purchasing of Goods and Services
Procurement of goods and services should provide good value for money spent through a transparent
process. The procedures outlined in below describes the procurements steps:
• Specify goods or services to be purchased, check budget: The standard, quantity and price of goods or
services required, as described in the activity plans, is specified clearly. The amount currently available in
the budget for the item to be purchased should be checked at the specification stage in case the price has
changed since the budget was prepared.
• Prepare Purchase Requisition An internal request is prepared (usually on a standard form for that purpose)
to formally request the purchase of the goods or services specified. The request will include a description of
the purchase and state why it is required.
• Authorize Purchase Requisition The purchase requisition will usually be checked and authorized by the
budget holder or other nominated person to verify that there is a genuine reason for the purchase. The
available budget will usually be checked again at this stage.
• Obtain Quotations from reputable independent suppliers are requested (in accordance with internal
procedures and donor rules) to make sure the organization gets best value for money and to minimize the
risk of collusion.
• Select Supplier Quotations are reviewed and a supplier is selected based on price, quality, delivery times
and ‘after sales’ terms to ensure value for money. For larger purchases, it is usual to have a Procurement
Committee (comprising of a small group of staff and WDC members) who take responsibility for selecting
the supplier.
• Issue (Authorized) Purchase Order (PO) The authorized Purchase Order is sent to the selected supplier
with a copy kept on file with the supplier’s quotation. This is a legally binding contract.
is usually signed to confirm receipt and a copy filed for later reference.
• Receive and Check Supplier Invoice The invoice should be checked and matched up with the GRN, PO and
quotation, usually by the finance team.
SECTION EIGHT
• Receive Goods from Supplier When supplies are delivered and received, a Goods Received Note (GRN)
PAGE 388
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
• Prepare and Authorize Payment Authority The Payment Authority is attached to the invoice and all the
supporting documents. It includes budget and accounting codes and must be checked and authorized by the
budget holder or other nominated person.
• Pay Supplier Invoice Payment should be made to the supplier within the specified payment terms, usually
30 days.
• Enter Payment into Cashbook The final stage is to record the payment in the books of account.
8.2.4 Sundry Finance Functions: Banking Functions
Opening Bank Accounts
It is advisable that the selected bank is easily accessible from the location of the PHC, to this end, account
must be opened with a Bank located or having a branch in the local government area where the PHC is
located. Where this is not possible, then the bank must be in the nearest local government area. The account
to be opened must be in the name of the PHC. Follow the following procedures:
• Obtain authorization letter from the SPHCDB authorizing the opening of the bank account and stating the
signatories to the account.
• Obtain accounts opening form from the Bank.
• Fill the account opening form: Name of Account (health facility name), address, type of account (corporate),
specify telephone number to receive bank alerts (this should be a person separate from the Accounts Clerk),
request for cheque book, online banking access.
• Fill the bank Mandate cards for the account (The account operating mandate to the bank should consist of
a combination of signatories of categories A and B: the “A” signatories shall be from the health facility; these
will be the OIC (main) and another staff from the same facility (alternate). The “B” signatories shall be from
the WDC, these will be the WDC Chair (main) and the WDC Treasurer (alternate).
• Obtain passport photographs and copies of valid means of identification (National Identity Card, Driver’s
License, International Passport or Voter’s Card) of each of the signatories in panel A and B.
• Submit account opening form, completed mandate cards, copies of means of identification of signatories,
SECTION EIGHT
copies of PHC letter headed paper (copy of which future instructions from the PHC to the bank will be
made) to the bank for processing.
Changing Signatories to a Bank Account
This could happen in a situation where one or more authorized signatories of the PHC bank account are no
longer available or unable to perform their duties. Follow the following steps:
•Obtain letter from the SPHCDB authorizing change of signatories, stating the name of the signatory that
needs to be changed and the new one(s) as replacement(s).
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
PAGE 389
•Obtain the passport photograph and copies of valid means of identification of the new signatory.
• Fill a new mandate card and capture the signature of the new signatory on the card.
• Submit SPHCDB letter, completed new mandate card and other documents (copies of valid means of
identification) to the bank.
Closing a Bank Account
• Obtain a letter from the SPHCD/A authorizing closure of the PHC account.
• Authorized signatories sign a letter requesting the bank to close the account.
• Present documents in steps 1 and 2 to the bank to effect closure of the account.
Requesting Bank Statements
•Prepare letter using the PHC letterhead addressed to the Bank Manager requesting the Bank Statement and
stating the period for which the statement is required.
•Get authorized signatories to sign the request letter.
• Present the signed request letter to the Bank.
Making Bank Transfer Instructions
• Prepare a letter addressed to the bank using the PHC letterhead instructing the bank to transfer funds
based on the schedule included of beneficiary(ies) showing names, account numbers, beneficiary bank and
amount to be transferred to each.
• Get the authorized signatories to sign the letter according to the bank mandate. – Present letter to the
bank and obtain signed and Stamped (acknowledged) copy.
• Confirm debit alert with transfer instruction.
Making Payments
Depending on volume of transactions and to reduce frequency of traffic to the bank, the Accounts Clerk
may schedule two days of the week when payments are made. He/she will compile payments scheduled to be
made so the approvals and instructions to the bank are made once or twice in the week.
Step 1. Compile vendor documents (approved invoice, purchase order or instruction to provide services of
supplies ensuring supplies are in line with the purchase order/instructions in terms of quantity and quality),
or service provided certified to be satisfactory by the user or consumer of the service)
Step 3. Attach documents in step 1 to the PV raised in step 2 ensuring adequacy and appropriateness of the
supporting documents and present to the OIC for approval.
Step 4. Record the name of beneficiary, amount date and cheque number and PV number in the Cheque
register and raise cheque in the name of the beneficiary (same name on the invoice) where payment is to
be made with cheque. If payment is to be made by bank transfer, prepare instruction to the bank on PHC
SECTION EIGHT
Step 2. Raise payment voucher (PV) showing the name of the beneficiary (vendor), amount (in words and
figures), purpose of payment, date of payment voucher and the PV number.
PAGE 390
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
letterhead stating the beneficiary’s details.
Step 5. Present the cheque or bank transfer instruction to the authorized signatories for signing.
Step 6. Get vendor to acknowledge receipt of the cheque on the copy of the cheque (stating “original
cheque collected by me”) or on the cheque register showing name of person collecting the cheque, date of
collection and signature.
Preparing the Bank Reconciliation Statement
The PHCs financial transactions are recorded by both the bank where the transactions take place and
the PHC itself. The bank records these transactions in the bank statement) while the PHC records in the
Cashbook, which means that the same transactions are recorded in in different books by different entities
(the Bank and the PHC). It is necessary to prepare a reconciliation of both records to ensure that all
transactions are captured in both books for completeness and accuracy and to prevent financial fraud. Bank
reconciliation statements are usually prepared monthly, but they can be prepared weekly or daily depending
on the volume of transactions. Maintain a file for bank reconciliations and bank statements. Follow the steps
below in preparing the bank reconciliation statement.
• Obtain the bank statement for the period for which the bank reconciliation is to be prepared.
• Tick off the items on the credit side of bank statement (that is incomes) against the items on the left-hand
side of the cashbook. Note items outstanding on both records as follows:
• Items outstanding on the credit side of bank statement but not in the Cashbook will be: i. Direct deposit
or transfers into the bank account that the PHC is yet to recognize in the cash book. Update the cashbook
and the Ledger Account of the depositor.
• Items outstanding on the left-hand side of the cashbook but not on the bank statement will be: i. Cheques
received but not yet deposited in the bank.
• Tick off items on the debit side of the bank statement (that is expenses) against items on the right-hand
side of the cashbook, note any outstanding items.
• Items outstanding on the debit side of bank statement but not on cashbook will be: i. Bank charges
(maintenance fee, sms alert charge, cheque book charge etc) for which the PHC will only be aware when it
receives the bank statement. Enter these in the cashbook before closing the book for the month and update
the Expense ledger account.
• Items outstanding on the right-hand side of the cashbook but not on the bank statement will be: i. Payments
SECTION EIGHT
made such as cheques issued to vendors by the PHC which are yet to be presented for payment.

HEALTH INFORMATION SYSTEM (HIS) FOR PHCs
8.3.1 Background to Health Information System (HIS)
Health Information System (HIS) is an integral and major thrust of any health system.The goal of the National
Health Management Information System (NHMIS) is to establish an effective Health Management Information
System at all levels of government in the Federation. The NHMIS provides information on health service
delivery and the health system that supports it, in a timely and reliable manner. It is equipped to improve data
capture, storage, analysis and report generation for health data in Nigeria.
Nigeria uses DHIS2 as the instance for reporting health data at all levels of government and facilities in
the country. There are currently several forms available on the NHMIS DHIS platform. These paper-based
monthly reports are collated and populated by the M&E focal person of the health facility for the previous
month and is submitted to the LGA office by the 7th day of the current month which is the deadline for
submission. The submitted information is then entered into the DHIS2 system by the M&E focal person at
the LGA health office by the 14th day of the current month. To enhance data accuracy, validation rules have
been set up in DHIS2. Tracer data elements have been made compulsory before data can be submitted to
enhance data completeness.
1.
Health data are captured from the clients at the health facility (outpatient or inpatient)
and recorded on the service forms.
2.
Data recorded on the service forms are immediately entered into the appropriate health
facility registers.
Steps 1 and 2 are done by the health workers who are providing the health services.
The facility in-charge should ensure that the right HMIS tools are available and used by
the health workers collecting health data in Steps 1 and 2.
3.
The facility in-charge or designated health facility staff should obtain the different registers
and respective tally sheets to tally the recorded health data and generate daily summaries.
Make sure that no field in the register is left blank. Include total number of clients served
for each service delivery area for every given month to the relevant service report for the
month.
4.
For any unclear information, the staff completing the tool should consult the health worker
before the end of the day to verify the information recorded in the registers. Check
whether the client is an old or new. If it is a new client, assign a client number and issue
folder and proceed with registration. If it is an old client, locate the folder number, retrieve
the folder, and proceed with registration.
5.
Use appropriate standard registers to register the client. Record details of the client using
indelible ink/pen with sufficient detail; ensure details are readable. Record in legible
handwriting. Record all fields in the register.
SECTION EIGHT
All Health facilities report through these HMIS tools, and no other person, project, or partner is allowed to
update, add, or edit the HMIS tools or introduce parallel data collection and reporting tools without approval.
At the health facility levels, the following steps are involved in HMIS data collection:
PAGE 392
NATIONAL STANDING ORDERS FOR COMMUNITY HEALTH OFFICERS
Steps Involved in Reporting and Transmitting HMIS Data
The steps are detailed below:
1.
Using the daily tally sheet, compile the weekly and monthly health summary reports.
2.
The facility in-charge or the ward in-charge (in case of hospital) should cross-check the
weekly or monthly HMIS reports for errors before submission to the next level.
3.
Entries are to be made and regularly updated in the health facility DHIS2 databases at the
LGA level.
4.
Transmission of reporting forms to the next levels are done within the following timeframes:
•
Hospital wards/units to M&E units: second day of each month.
•
Health facility to LGA: seventh day of each month.
•
LGA to national: 14th day of each month
•
National to programs and partners: 25th day of the ensuing month
SECTION EIGHT
Steps Involved in Validating HMIS Data Before transmitting HMIS data to the next level
Each preceding level should cross-check the weekly or monthly health summary forms for errors. Specifically,
the data validation should follow these steps:
1.
Form a data validation team at each level. At the health facility, the facility in-charge should
be the chairperson of this team.
2.
Establish a culture of routine data validation, recounts, and verification.
3.
Meet monthly to validate data before transmission.
4.
Cross-check total figures on the reporting forms.
5.
Check for accuracy and completeness of reports.
6.
Cross-check data consistency across reports.
7.
Look for unusually low or high values for events and diseases.
8.
Compare with previous months and the same period the year before.
9.
Do necessary corrections before transmission.
10.
Sign off on the reports after they are validated by the facility in-charge.

8.4 Health Management Information Systems (HMIS)
Health Management Information Systems (HMIS) are one of the six building blocks essential for health system
strengthening. HMIS is a data collection system specifically designed to support planning, management, and
decision making in health facilities and organizations.
MEASURE Evaluation worked in Ethiopia to implement a HMIS in the Southern Nations, Nationalities, and
Peoples’ Region (SNNPR) and Oromia.The following training materials were used to support implementation
of the HMIS including data quality assurance and information use procedures, the electronic HMIS system for
managing data processing, reporting, analysis and use; and the and the community health information system
(CHIS), which organizes information on individuals and families—information related to vaccines, family
planning, maternal and child health, HIV treatment and support, and other services—and makes it available
all in one place, such as a family folder.
The National Health Management Information System is intended to provide information on Health Service
delivery and the health system that supports it in a timely and reliable manner.The efficiency and effectiveness
of health services delivery is assessed by availability of quality, and timely information.
A. Routine Data collection summary forms
1. NHMS 000: Community based summary forms
This is used by the community Village Health worker to summarize activities at monthly intervals sent to the
health facility through the Community Health Extension worker (CHEW)
Revised NHMIS forms 2
2. NHMIS summary forms 001A & 001B
• Health facility community outreach forms
• Used by the health facilities to summarize community outreach activities at monthly intervals
• Aggregate transferred to health facility form 001
• Revised NHMIS forms 3
3. NHMIS summary form 001
• Health facility based forms
• Used by health facility to summarize activities in the health facility at monthly intervals from a daily register
• Sent to the LGA
• Revised NHMIS forms 4
4. NHMIS summary form 002
5. NHMIS summary form 003
• Used by the state to summarize data from all LGAs.
• Sent to the Federal NHMIS every 6 months.
SECTION EIGHT
• Used by the LGAs to summarize data from all health facilities in their area at quarterly intervals.
• Sent to the SMOH, HMIS unit.
